\epsilon\in S^{\prime}\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{D}
, by equation (
3.10
) we have

\commutator{X_A}{\epsilon}_{p}=(A_{X_{A}})_{p}\cdot\epsilon_{p}=A\cdot\epsilon=\commutator{A}{\epsilon},

so
\commutator{A}{\epsilon}\in S^{\prime}
. Thus
\commutator{\fh}{S'}\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Finally, for
\epsilon,\zeta\in S
, we have

\commutator{\epsilon}{\zeta}_{p}=\kappa_{p}(\epsilon_{p},\zeta_{p})=\commutator{\epsilon_p}{\zeta_p}
,
so
\commutator{S'}{S'}\subseteq V^{\prime}
. Hence
\mathfrak{a}
is indeed a graded Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
.

Using the notation and terminology of Appendices
A.3.2
and
A.3.3
, we can equip
\mathfrak{K}_{D}
with a filtration
\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}
of depth 2 (
\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{i}=\mathfrak{K}_{D}
for
i\leq-2
) as follows: we set
\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{i}=0
for
i>0
and

\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{-2}=\mathfrak{K}_{D}=\mathfrak{V}_{D}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}\quad\supset\quad\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{-1}=\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}\quad\supset\quad\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{0}=\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\cong\mathfrak{h}.

The calculations above show that, as
\mathfrak{h}\cong\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}
-modules,

\displaystyle\Gr_{-2}\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathfrak{K}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.45613pt}{$\textstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.7728pt}{$\scriptstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.7728pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}}}\cong V^{\prime}\qquad\text{\big(using $\evaluate^{V}_{p}$\big)},

\displaystyle\Gr_{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}}$}}{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\textstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.45613pt}{$\textstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}}$}}{\raisebox{3.7728pt}{$\scriptstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.7728pt}{$\scriptstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}}$}}{\raisebox{3.7728pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.7728pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}}$}}}\cong S^{\prime}\qquad\text{\big(using $\evaluate^{S}_{p}$\big)},

\displaystyle\Gr_{0}\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}=\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}\cong\mathfrak{h}\qquad\text{(using $X\mapsto(A_{X})_{p}$)},

thus
\Gr\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}\cong\mathfrak{a}
as
\mathfrak{h}
-modules. We will show that this isomorphism preserves Lie brackets, hence
\mathfrak{K}_{D}
is a filtered deformation of
\mathfrak{a}
. We denote the associated graded bracket on
\Gr\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}
by
\commutator{-}{-}_{\mathrm{Gr}}
and use an overline to denote projections
\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{i}\to\Gr\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{i}
. For
A,B\in\mathfrak{h}
,
X,Y\in\mathfrak{V}_{D}
and
\epsilon,\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}
, we have the following for our map
\Gr\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}\to\mathfrak{a}
:

\displaystyle\commutator{X_A}{\overline{Y}}_{\mathrm{Gr}}=\overline{\commutator{X_A}{Y}}=\overline{A_{X_{A}}Y}\longmapsto AY_{p}=\commutator{A}{Y_p}\qquad(\text{in degree $-2$}),

\displaystyle\commutator{\epsilon}{\zeta}_{\mathrm{Gr}}=\overline{\commutator{\epsilon}{\zeta}}=\overline{\kappa(\epsilon,\zeta)}\longmapsto\kappa_{p}(\epsilon,\zeta)=\commutator{\epsilon}{\zeta}\qquad(\text{in degree $-2$}),

\displaystyle\commutator{X_A}{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{Gr}}=\overline{\commutator{X_A}{\epsilon}}=\overline{A_{X_{A}}\cdot\epsilon}\longmapsto A\cdot\epsilon=\commutator{A}{\epsilon}\qquad(\text{in degree $-1$}),

\displaystyle\commutator{X_A}{X_B}_{\mathrm{Gr}}=\overline{\commutator{X_A}{X_B}}=X_{\commutator{A}{B}}\longmapsto\commutator{A}{B}\qquad(\text{in degree $0$}).

The only brackets we have not considered above are
\big[\overline{X},\epsilon\big]_{\mathrm{Gr}}
and
\big[\overline{X},\overline{Y}\big]_{\mathrm{Gr}}
, but these lie in
\Gr_{-3}\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}
and
\Gr_{-4}\mathfrak{K}_{D}^{\bullet}
respectively, both of which are both zero by definition.
∎

We recall from the discussion in Section
2.2.2
that filtered deformations can be explicitly described as a deformation of the bracket of the graded model algebra by a sequence of maps of positive degrees. It will be useful to give our Killing superalgebras such a presentation.

First note that the isomorphism
\mathfrak{h}\cong\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}
and the evaluation map
\evaluate^{V}_{p}
itself give us the short exact sequence of vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{V}_{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Any short exact sequence of vector spaces splits, so there exists a (linear) splitting map
\Lambda\colon V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{V}_{D}
with
\evaluate^{V}_{p}(\Lambda(v))=\Lambda(v)_{p}=v
. We can also parametrise the splitting in terms of a map
\lambda\colon V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
by setting
\lambda(v)=(A_{\Lambda(v)})_{p}
; the vector field
\Lambda(v)
can be recovered from its Killing transport data
(v,\lambda(v))
. The splitting induces a linear isomorphism
V^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{V}_{D}
given by
(v,A)\mapsto\Lambda(v)+X_{A}
.
This extends to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded linear isomorphism
\Psi\colon\mathfrak{a}\to\mathfrak{K}_{D}
given by
{\Psi(v,\epsilon,A)=(\Lambda(v)+X_{A},\epsilon)}
,
where
v\in V^{\prime}
,
A\in\mathfrak{h}
, and
\epsilon\in S^{\prime}\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{D}
.
We use
\Psi
to define a new bracket
\commutator{-}{-}^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{a}
by pulling back the bracket on
\mathfrak{K}_{D}

\commutator{(v,\epsilon,A)}{(w,\zeta,B)}^{\prime}:=\Psi^{-1}(\commutator{\Psi(v,\epsilon,A)}{\Psi(w,\zeta,B)})

with respect to which
\Psi
is a Lie (super)algebra isomorphism
(\mathfrak{a},\commutator{-}{-}^{\prime})\cong\mathfrak{K}_{D}
by construction. Explicitly, the bracket is given by

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{B}^{\prime}=\commutator{A}{B},\qquad\commutator{A}{v}^{\prime}=Av+\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(Av),

\displaystyle\commutator{v}{w}^{\prime}=\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v+\theta_{\lambda}(v,w),\qquad\commutator{A}{\epsilon}^{\prime}=A\cdot\epsilon,

\displaystyle\commutator{v}{\epsilon}^{\prime}=\beta_{p}(v)\epsilon+\lambda(v)\cdot\epsilon,\qquad\commutator{\epsilon}{\zeta}^{\prime}=\kappa_{p}(\epsilon,\zeta)+\gamma_{p}(\epsilon,\zeta)-\lambda\quantity(\kappa_p(\epsilon,\zeta)),

(3.12)

where
\gamma_{p}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigotimes}}{\bigotimes}{\bigotimes}{\bigotimes}^{2}S\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
is the evaluation at
p
of the map
\gamma
defined by equation (
3.3
) and
\theta_{\lambda}:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}
is the map

\theta_{\lambda}(v,w)=\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\lambda(w)}-R_{p}(v,w)-\lambda(\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v).

Using the notation of Lie algebra cohomology, we have a map
\partial\lambda\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}\mathfrak{s}\to\mathfrak{s}
of degree
+2
defined as follows

\displaystyle\partial\lambda(A,v)=\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(Av),\qquad\partial\lambda(v,w)=\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v,\qquad\partial\lambda(v,\epsilon)=\lambda(v)\cdot\epsilon,

\displaystyle\partial\lambda(\epsilon,\zeta)=-\lambda(\kappa_{p}(\epsilon,\zeta)),

whence we have

\commutator{-}{-}^{\prime}=\commutator{-}{-}+\beta_{p}+\gamma_{p}+\partial\lambda+\theta_{\lambda},

where
\beta_{p}
,
\gamma_{p}
and
\theta_{\lambda}
have been trivially extended to maps
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}\mathfrak{s}\to\mathfrak{s}
. The deforming maps are
\mu=\beta_{p}+\gamma_{p}+\partial\lambda
, of degree
+2
, and
\theta_{\lambda}
, of degree
+4
. That is, in the language of Section
2.2.2
(see equation (
2.5
)), the deformation has defining sequence
(\mu,\theta_{\lambda},0,\dots)
.

The splitting
\Lambda\colon V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{V}_{D}
and the corresponding map
\lambda\colon V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}
can be viewed as a “correction” to the failed naïve approach discussed in the remark before the proof of Theorem
3.10
. Note that these maps are not canonical; if
\Lambda^{\prime}\colon V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{V}_{D}
is another splitting and
\lambda^{\prime}\colon V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
is the corresponding map, we have

\Phi_{p}(\Lambda^{\prime}(v)-\Lambda(v))=(v-v,\lambda^{\prime}(v)-\lambda(v))=(0,\lambda^{\prime}(v)-\lambda(v)),

so
(\Lambda^{\prime}-\Lambda)(v)\in\ker\evaluate^{V}_{p}
, and
(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda)(v)
is the corresponding element of
\mathfrak{h}
. Thus
\lambda
, and therefore the splitting, is unique up to a choice of map
V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}
. We will see later that such a map is a Spencer
(2,1)
-cocycle for
\mathfrak{a}
, so it is consistent that changing the defining sequence by the coboundary of such a map does not change the isomorphism class of the deformation, only its presentation. Also note that this choice of splitting is exactly a choice of complementary submodules as discussed near the beginning of Section
2.2.2
.

3.3
Constrained Killing spinors

So far in this section, we have only considered the differential Killing spinor equation
{D\epsilon=0}
which in supergravity theories arises from demanding that the variation of the gravitino
{\partial_{\epsilon}\Psi=\!D\epsilon}
vanishes. While in some pure minimal supergravity theories (in particular, those in dimensions 4, 5, 6 and
11
, which have been studied using Spencer cohomology
[
10
,
22
,
23
,
31
,
33
]
) this is the only local condition imposed by supersymmetry, in most supergravity theories (including any extended, gauged or matter-coupled theories and even some pure minimal theories), there are additional fermions present. A background of such a theory is called supersymmetric if the variations of
all
fermions vanish for some supersymmetry parameter. The variations of the additional fermions are always algebraic, rather than differential, in the spinor. The vanishing of these variations are referred to as
algebraic
or
linear constraints
and we will call the spinors satisfying them as well as
D\epsilon=0

constrained Killing spinors
.
15
15

15

Our terminology follows
[
18
,
48
]
(omitting the word “generalised”). The more recent work
[
59
]
uses the term “constrained differential spinor”.

Similar considerations for Killing superalgebras are found in
[
42
]
.

We will make some comments here about how these might be brought into our present framework; we leave a full treatment for future work.

3.3.1
Linear constraints

Schematically, the linear constraints are of the form
\mathscr{P}\epsilon=0
where
\mathscr{P}
is some fibrewise linear operator on the spinor bundle
\underline{S}
. More concretely, all fermions may take values in the same representation as the gravitini, in which case
\mathscr{P}\in\End\underline{S}
. In a more general situation, the additional fermions take values in some vector bundle
E\to M
(for example, an associated bundle for some gauge symmetry). Then the constraint equation is
\mathscr{P}\epsilon=0
, where

\mathscr{P}\in\Hom(\underline{S},\underline{S}\otimes E)\cong\End\underline{S}\otimes E.

This bundle map is parametrised by the bosonic fields of the background, just as
{\beta=D-\nabla}
is.
16
16

16

Let us note that the fermion variations
\mathscr{P}\epsilon
and the expression
\beta\epsilon
are fibrewise linear in
\epsilon
but not necessarily in the bosonic background fields. For example, gauge field strengths (hence derivatives of gauge connections) and derivatives of scalars typically appear as coefficients in both, and may also appear in nonlinear combinations.

Let
D=\nabla-\beta
be a spinor connection which is not necessarily admissible in the sense of Definition
3.6
and let us denote the space of
D
-parallel sections of
\underline{S}
which are annihilated by
\mathscr{P}
by

\mathfrak{S}_{D,\mathscr{P}}:=\quantity{\epsilon\in\fS\mid D\epsilon= 0 \text{ and } \eP\epsilon= 0}.

We previously discussed the integrability condition
R^{D}\epsilon=0
for the existence of
D
-parallel spinors (see equation (
3.1
) and the subsequent discussion). In the case of constrained Killing spinors
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{S}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
, this can be supplemented by further integrability conditions, the first-order such conditions being schematically (see, e.g.,
[
39
]
)

\commutator{\eP}{\eP}\epsilon=0
,
\widehat{D}\mathscr{P}\epsilon:=\commutator{D}{\eP}\epsilon=0
,
where
{\commutator{-}{-}}
in both expressions is a commutator. These conditions require some interpretation which depends on the structure on the bundle
E
in which the additional fermions take their values. If
\mathscr{P}\in\End\underline{S}
, so
E\to M
is the trivial line bundle, then the meaning of the expressions is clear. The same holds if
E
is simply the sum of such bundles. In the more general situation, we may interpret
\mathscr{P}
as acting on
\underline{S}\otimes E
as
\mathscr{P}\otimes\Id_{E}
and extend
D
to a connection on
\underline{S}\otimes E
which acts trivially on the second factor. In practice though, there may be some more natural interpretation in which these operations are “twisted” by some structure on
E
.

3.3.2
Constrained Killing superalgebra

As we did for
\mathfrak{S}_{D}
in Section
3.1
, we now seek to find a subalgebra of the algebra of Killing vectors which preserves this space under the action of the Lie derivative; a natural choice is

\mathfrak{V}_{D,\mathscr{P}}:=\big\{X\in\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)\mid\mathscr{L}_{X}\beta=0\text{ and }\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{X}\mathscr{P}=0\big\},

where again we must interpret the expression
\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{X}\mathscr{P}
. In particular, we assume that there is some structure on
E
such which gives rise to some generalisation
\widehat{\mathscr{L}}
of the Lie derivative which acts on
\Hom\underline{S}\otimes E
– if
E
happens to be a trivial bundle, the spinorial Lie derivative naturally generalises, while if it is an associated bundle to the principal bundle for some gauge symmetry, a covariant derivative constructed from a gauge connection will suffice. We note that in the latter case,
\mathfrak{V}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
may not close under the Lie bracket, so we may need to refine its definition.

If
\mathfrak{V}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
(or some more carefully chosen subspace) is indeed a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
, its action via the spinorial Lie derivative preserves
\mathfrak{S}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
.
We now seek to give
\mathfrak{K}_{D,\mathscr{P}}=\mathfrak{V}_{D,\mathscr{P}}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
the structure of a superalgebra using the Lie derivative and Dirac current as in Theorem
3.5
. But as in the proof of that theorem, all that needs to be checked is that the
\commutator{\fS_{D,\eP}}{\fS_{D,\eP}}
bracket closes on
\mathfrak{V}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
, and that the
\quantity[\fS_{D,\eP},\fS_{D,\eP},\fS_{D,\eP}]
Jacobi identity is satisfied. But we quickly see that this is the case if equations (
3.4
)–(
3.6
) as well as

\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\kappa(\epsilon,\zeta)}\mathscr{P}=0

(3.13)

are satisfied for all
\epsilon,\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
. We note that in a supergravity theory,
\beta
and
\mathscr{P}
are defined in terms of background fields which we expect to be preserved by the Lie derivatives along Killing vectors generated by spinors in
\mathfrak{S}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
, hence the condition above is also satisfied.

Assuming that the expressions above can be made precise as suggested, we should then generalise Definition
3.6
to the constrained case, considering not just admissible connections
D
but admissible
pairs

(D,\mathscr{P})
, incorporating the condition (
3.13
) and possibly demanding that the admissibility conditions of Definition
3.6
should only be imposed on
\ker\mathscr{P}
, rather than the whole spinor bundle.

3.3.3
Killing transport and filtered deformation

We now consider whether an analogue of Theorem
3.10
holds, that is, whether it is the case that the Killing superalgebra is a filtered deformation of the Poincaré superalgebra in the constrained case. Recall that an important part of this proof was the localisation of the Killing vectors and spinors in
\mathfrak{K}_{D}
at a point via Killing transport. Since constrained Killing spinors are still
D
-parallel with respect to a connection
D
, the same arguments hold, the only modification being that the Killing transport data of a constrained Killing spinor at
p\in M
must lie in
\ker\mathscr{P}_{p}
. Note that this does not mean that any spinor at
p
annihilated by
\mathscr{P}
can be Killing-transported to define a constrained Killing spinor; as in the unconstrained case, a
D
-parallel global section with this transport data may not exist, and in the constrained case, even if one does exist, it need not be
\mathscr{P}
-parallel.

One should thus expect an analogue to Theorem
3.10
for
\mathfrak{K}_{D,\mathscr{P}}
with essentially the same proof, the main difference being that the filtered subdeformation of
\mathfrak{s}
one thus obtains must have
S^{\prime}\simeq\evaluate^{S}_{p}\mathfrak{S}_{D,\mathscr{P}}\subseteq\ker\mathscr{P}_{p}\subseteq\underline{S}_{p}
.

We will not consider constrained Killing spinors in the rest of this work, primarily because, in contrast to the differential Killing spinor equation, the linear constraints apparently do not have an interpretation in terms of Lie algebra cohomology. We thus leave the details of the formalism suggested above to be fully treated in future publications.

4
Filtered subdeformations of the Poincaré superalgebra

Theorem
3.10
establishes that any Killing (super)algebra is a filtered deformation of a graded subalgebra of a flat model (super)algebra
\mathfrak{s}
(see Definition
2.1
). Let us now consider such deformations from a homological point of view. Throughout, let
(V,\eta)
be an inner product space and let
S
be a (possibly
N
-extended) spinor module of
\Spin(V)
. For ease of exposition, we now specialise to the case of a symmetric Dirac current
\kappa\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S\to V
, whence we work with
super
algebras; the skew-symmetric case is entirely analogous. Eventually, we will further specialise to Lorentzian signature, hence Poincaré superalgebras, and make some further assumptions on the Dirac current and the graded subalgebras we work with in order to take advantage of some technical simplifications offered by the homogeneity theorem (see Theorem
4.8
). We comment on how dependent our results are on these assumptions in Remark
4.11
. We will make extensive use of the background material on Spencer cohomology and filtered deformations presented in Section
2.2
.

4.1
The Spencer (2,2)-cohomology

We saw in Section
2.2.2
that filtered deformations of graded Lie superalgebras are governed by their Spencer cohomology. We therefore need to understand the Spencer cohomology of the flat model superalgebra and its graded subalgebras before discussing their deformations.

4.1.1
The complex

A graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
of the flat model superalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
defined by the data
(V,S,\kappa)
takes the form
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{0}
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{-1}=S^{\prime}
is a vector subspace of
S
,
\mathfrak{a}_{-2}=V^{\prime}
is a subspace of
V
containing
\imaginary\kappa|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=\mathfrak{h}
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
preserving
S^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}
. By Proposition
2.3
, to study filtered deformations of
\mathfrak{a}
we must first understand the degree-2 Spencer complex
\bigl(\mathsf{C}^{2,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a}),\partial\bigr)
. Recalling the definition of the Spencer complex from Section
2.2.1
, the space of
(2,p)
-cochains consists of degree-2 maps
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{p}\mathfrak{a}\to\mathfrak{a}
, whence we deduce that the degree-2 complex can be written as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Hom(V^{\prime},\mathfrak{h})\longrightarrow\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V^{\prime},V^{\prime}\bigr)\oplus\Hom(V^{\prime}\otimes S^{\prime},S^{\prime})\oplus\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Hom\bigl(V^{\prime}\otimes\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime},V^{\prime}\bigr)\oplus\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{3}S^{\prime},S^{\prime}\bigr)\longrightarrow 0.

We denote the projections to the components of
\mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
as follows

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\colon\ \mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})\longrightarrow\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V^{\prime},V^{\prime}\bigr),\qquad\pi_{2}\colon\ \mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})\longrightarrow\Hom(V\otimes S^{\prime},S^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\pi_{3}\colon\ \mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})\longrightarrow\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}\bigr).

We now record a pair of results which will be useful for studying the cohomology of a class of graded subalgebras which we will be particularly interested in later on.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
and
V^{\prime}
an
\mathfrak{h}
-submodule of
V
such that

(1)

the restriction of
\eta
to
V^{\prime}
is non-degenerate,

(2)

the action of
\mathfrak{h}
on
V^{\prime}
is faithful,

(
which hold in particular for
V^{\prime}=V)
and suppose that
\lambda\colon V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}
is a linear map such that

\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v=0,\qquad\forall v,w\in V^{\prime}.

Then
\lambda=0
.

Proof.

Using that
\lambda
takes values in
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
, for all
u,v,w\in V^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\eta(u,\lambda(v)w)

\displaystyle=\eta(u,\lambda(w)v)=-\eta(\lambda(w)u,v)=-\eta(\lambda(u)w,v)

\displaystyle=\eta(w,\lambda(u)v)=\eta(w,\lambda(v)u)=-\eta(\lambda(v)w,u).

Thus by non-degeneracy we have
\lambda(v)w=0
for all
v,w\in V^{\prime}
, so since
\mathfrak{h}
acts faithfully on
V^{\prime}
,
\lambda=0
.
∎

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}=V\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
be a graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
with
\mathfrak{a}_{-2}=V
. Then the composition

\pi_{1}\circ\partial\colon\Hom(V,\mathfrak{h})\to\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V,V\bigr)

is injective, whence
{\mathsf{H}^{2,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=\mathsf{Z}^{2,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0}
. If
{\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{so}(V)}
,
\pi_{1}\circ\partial
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\ker(\pi_{1}\circ\partial)
. Then for all
v,w\in V
,

(\pi_{1}\circ\partial)(\lambda)(v,w)=\partial\lambda(v,w)=\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v=0
.
The first claim then follows from Lemma
4.1
. The second claim follows by a dimension count.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

The second claim in the result above can be restated as follows: for any linear map
{\alpha\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to V}
, there exists a unique linear map
\lambda\colon V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
satisfying
\alpha(v,w)=\partial\lambda(v,w)=\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v
. We will use this fact repeatedly.

4.1.2
The cohomology

The discussion above gives us a characterisation of the
(2,2)
-cohomology group of the whole flat model superalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
which we will later be able to bootstrap in order to study a particular class of filtered subdeformations.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\alpha+\beta+\gamma
be a cocycle in
\mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
where

\displaystyle\alpha\in\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V,V\bigr),\qquad\beta\in\Hom(V\otimes S,S),\qquad\gamma\in\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S,\mathfrak{so}(V)\bigr).

(4.1)

Then the cohomology class
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
has a unique representative
\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}
with no
\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V,V\bigr)
component. We denote the space of such
normalised cocycles
by
\mathcal{H}^{2,2}

\mathcal{H}^{2,2}=\quantity{\beta+\gamma\in\ssZ^{2,2}(\fs_-;\fs)
\mid\beta\in\Hom(V\otimes S,S), \gamma\in\Hom\big(\Odot^2S,\fso(V)\big)
},

and then we have

\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathcal{H}^{2,2}

as
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-modules.

Proof.

Using the isomorphism
\pi_{1}\circ\partial
, there exists a unique map
\lambda\in\Hom(V,\mathfrak{so}(V))
such that
{\partial\lambda(v,w)=\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v=\alpha(v,w)}
.
Thus, if we define
\beta^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(v,s)=\beta(v,s)-\partial\lambda(v,s)=\beta(v,s)-\lambda(v)\cdot s,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}(s,s^{\prime})=\gamma(s,s^{\prime})-\partial\lambda(s,s^{\prime})=\gamma(s,s)+\lambda(\kappa(s,s^{\prime})),

we have

[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=[\alpha+\beta+\gamma-\partial\lambda]=[\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}]
,
which proves the first claim. It immediately follows that, as
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-modules, we have
\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})=\mathcal{H}^{2,2}\oplus\mathsf{B}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
and
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
.
∎

It is worth pausing to explicitly examine the “normalised” Spencer cocycle conditions for
\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
. Written in polarised form (explained in Section
2.2.3
), these are

\displaystyle 2\kappa(s,\beta(v,s))+\gamma(s,s)v=0,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\beta(\kappa_{s},s)+\gamma(s,s)\cdot s=0

(4.3)

for
v\in V
and
s\in S
, where we introduce the notation
\kappa_{s}:=\kappa(s,s)
. We will refer to these as the
first
and
second

(
normalised
)
Spencer cocycle conditions
for
\mathfrak{s}
respectively. We note that the first equation uniquely defines
\gamma\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
in terms of
\beta\colon V\otimes S\to S
and constrains the endomorphism
v\mapsto\kappa(s,\beta(v,s))
of
V
to lie in
\mathfrak{so}(V)
for all
s\in S
. In explicit cohomology calculations (see
[
10
,
22
,
23
,
33
]
), we first determine the space of maps
\beta
satisfying this constraint and then substitute the resulting expressions for
\beta
and
\gamma
into the second equation and solve it.

We also note here that
\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
(or the corresponding cohomology class) is invariant under the action of a subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathfrak{so}(V)
if and only if
\beta
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant (that is,
\beta
is
\mathfrak{h}
-equivariant when considered as a map
V\otimes S\to S
); indeed, if
A\in\mathfrak{h}
,
v\in V
and
s\in S
then we have
{(A\cdot(\beta+\gamma))(v,s)=(A\cdot\beta)(v,s)=A\cdot(\beta(v,s))-\beta(Av,s)-\beta(A,v\cdot s)}

so clearly
\beta
is invariant if
\beta+\gamma
is, while on the other hand if
\beta
is invariant then using the first cocycle condition (once in the form above and once in depolarised form) as well as
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-invariance of
\kappa
, we have

\displaystyle(A\cdot\gamma)(s,s)v

\displaystyle=\commutator{A}{\gamma(s,s)}v-2\gamma(A\cdot s,s)v

\displaystyle=2A(\kappa(s,\beta(v,s)))-2\kappa(s,\beta(Av,s))-2\kappa(A\cdot s,\beta(v,s))-2\kappa(s,\beta(v,A\cdot s))

\displaystyle=2\kappa(s,A\cdot\beta(v,s))-2\kappa(s,\beta(Av,s))-2\kappa(s,\beta(v,A\cdot s))=0,

thus
\gamma
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant, whence
\beta+\gamma
is too.

Remark 4.5
.

Let us pause briefly to relate these cocycle conditions back to our theory of Killing superalgebras and to explain the coincidences in notation. First note that there is a canonical homomorphism
\Hom(V\otimes S,S)\cong V^{*}\otimes\End S
, so we can view the cocycle component
\beta
as a spinor endomorphism-valued 1-form on
V
. Under this interpretation, we write
\beta(v)s:=\beta(v,s)
.

The definition of admissible connections, Definition
3.6
, involves three conditions on the 1-form
\beta=D-\nabla\in\Omega^{1}(M;\End\underline{S})
and the section
\gamma\in\Gamma\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}\underline{S}^{*}\otimes TM\bigr)
which is defined in terms of
\beta
by equation (
3.3
). Conditions (1) and (2) are algebraic in
\beta
,
\gamma
and condition (3) is differential. For a symmetric Dirac current, we can polarise the definition of
\gamma
and the algebraic conditions. Then, taking them pointwise, the definition of
\gamma
and condition (1) are equivalent to cocycle condition (
4.2
) above, while condition (2) is precisely (
4.3
) (where the fibre of
\underline{S}
is
S
and the same Dirac current is chosen). Thus, if we wish to find admissible connections on a spinor bundle
\underline{S}
equipped with a Dirac current
\kappa
over a spin manifold
(M,g)
, we need only compute
\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
to determine a local ansatz for the allowed 1-forms
\beta
and then check whether or under what circumstances the remaining condition (namely,
\mathscr{L}_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}\beta=0
for all
D
-parallel spinors
\epsilon
) holds, which we typically do using integrability conditions.

4.2
General filtered deformations and cohomology

Recall from Section
2.2.2
that we can describe filtered deformations of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded superalgebra as deformed brackets on the same underlying vector space as the original algebra. In particular, a filtered deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of a graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}=V^{\prime}\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{s}
has the brackets

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{B}=AB-BA,\qquad\commutator{s}{s}=\kappa_{s}+\gamma(s,s),\qquad\commutator{A}{v}=Av+\delta(A,v),

\displaystyle\commutator{v}{s}=\beta(v,s),\qquad\commutator{A}{s}=A\cdot s,\qquad\commutator{v}{w}=\alpha(v,w)+\theta(v,w),

(4.4)

where
A,B\in\mathfrak{h}
,
v,w\in V^{\prime}
,
s\in S^{\prime}
, and

\alpha\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
,
\beta\colon V^{\prime}\otimes S^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
,
\gamma\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}
,
\delta\colon\mathfrak{h}\otimes V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}

are the components of the degree-2 deformation map, while

\theta\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}

is the degree-4 deformation map. We denote the full degree-2 deformation by

{\mu=\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\delta\colon\mathfrak{a}\otimes\mathfrak{a}\to\mathfrak{a}}

so the defining sequence (as in equation (
2.5
)) for this deformation is
(\mu,\theta,0,\dots)
. From Proposition
2.3
, we have the following homological information:

\displaystyle\mu\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a};\mathfrak{a}),

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mu|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a}),

(4.6)

\displaystyle\quantity[\mu|_{\fa_-\otimes\fa_-}]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})^{\mathfrak{h}},

(4.7)

where in the first expression we have graded the full Chevalley–Eilenberg complex in the same way that we graded the Spencer complex in equation (
2.4
).

4.2.1
Unpacking the cocycle conditions

Let us consider these conditions in more detail. Equation (
4.5
) is equivalent to the following system of linear equations:

\displaystyle\alpha(\kappa_{s},v)+2\kappa(s,\beta(v,s))+\gamma(s,s)v=0,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\beta(\kappa_{s},s)+\gamma(s,s)\cdot s=0,

(4.9)

\displaystyle A\alpha(v,w)-\alpha(Av,w)-\alpha(v,Aw)+\delta(A,w)v-\delta(A,v)w=0,

(4.10)

\displaystyle A\cdot(\beta(v,s))-\beta(Av,s)-\beta(v,A\cdot s)-\delta(A,v)\cdot s=0,

(4.11)

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{\gamma(s,s)}-2\gamma(A\cdot s,s)+\delta(A,\kappa_{s})=0,

(4.12)

\displaystyle\delta(\commutator{A}{B},v)-\commutator{A}{\delta(B,v)}+\commutator{B}{\delta(A,v)}-\delta(A,Bv)+\delta(B,Av)=0

(4.13)

for all
A,B\in\mathfrak{h}
,
v,w\in V^{\prime}
and
s\in S^{\prime}
, where again
\kappa_{s}=\kappa(s,s)
. Equation (
4.6
) is equivalent to the first pair of equations in this system – we will refer to these as the
Spencer cocycle conditions
for
\mathfrak{a}
. Equation (
4.7
) is equivalent to the following: for each
A\in\mathfrak{h}
, there exists a cochain
\chi_{A}\in\mathsf{C}^{2,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=\Hom(V^{\prime},\mathfrak{h})
such that

A\cdot(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=\partial\chi_{A}.

(4.14)

This is implied by equation (
4.5
): simply taking
\chi_{A}=\imath_{A}\delta
, the above equation is equivalent to equations (
4.10
)–(
4.12
). In the case that
V^{\prime}=V
, we have a converse: it follows from Corollary
4.2
that (
4.14
) defines
\chi_{A}
uniquely and that the assignment
A\mapsto\chi_{A}
is linear. Thus we can define a unique linear map
\delta\colon\mathfrak{h}\otimes V\to\mathfrak{h}
by
\delta(A,v):=\chi_{A}(v)
. Then

\displaystyle\delta(\commutator{A}{B},v)-\commutator{A}{\delta(B,v)}+\commutator{B}{\delta(A,v)}-\delta(A,Bv)+\delta(B,Av)

\displaystyle\qquad=\chi_{\commutator{A}{B}}(v)-\commutator{A}{\chi_B(v)}+\commutator{B}{\chi_A(v)}-\chi_{A}(Bv)+\chi_{B}(Av)

\displaystyle\qquad=\quantity(\chi_{\comm{A}{B}} - A\cdot\chi_B + B\cdot\chi_A)(v).

On the other hand, by (
4.14
) we have

\partial(\chi_{\commutator{A}{B}}-A\cdot\chi_{B}+B\cdot\chi_{A})=0
,
so
\chi_{\commutator{A}{B}}-A\cdot\chi_{B}+B\cdot\chi_{A}\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
. But this space is zero by Corollary
4.2
. Hence
\delta
satisfies (
4.13
). Thus we have shown the following.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mu\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a};\mathfrak{a})
. Then
\mu|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
with
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant cohomology class. If
V^{\prime}=V
, we conversely have that if
\mu_{-}\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
is a Spencer cocycle such that with
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant cohomology class then there exists a unique cocycle
\delta\in\mathsf{Z}^{1}(\mathfrak{h};V^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h})\subseteq\Hom(\mathfrak{h}\otimes V,\mathfrak{h})
such that
\mu=\mu_{-}+\delta\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a};\mathfrak{a})
.

We can be more explicit about the form of
\delta
in the
V=V^{\prime}
case: by Corollary
4.2
, there exists a unique map
\lambda\colon V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
such that

\alpha(v,w)=\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v
.
One can then easily check that

\delta(A,v)=\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(Av)

solves equations (
4.10
)–(
4.12
).

4.2.2
Jacobi identities

We now consider the conditions imposed on the deforming maps by the Jacobi identities. These split into three types: those which are independent of the deforming maps, which are identically satisfied; those which involve only the deforming maps of degree 2, which are linear in those maps and which are equivalent to the homological conditions (
4.8
)–(
4.13
); and those which involve the degree-4 map
\theta
, all of which are quadratic in the deforming maps. We will not explicitly write the first two types, only the third type, since the equations are new. We denote by
[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{i},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}]
the Jacobi identity on the
(i,j,k)
-th homogeneous subspace of
\mathfrak{a}
. The Jacobi identities break down as follows:

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}]
: Identically satisfied by the Jacobi identity on
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
.

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1}]
: Identically satisfied since
S^{\prime}
is a representation of
\mathfrak{h}
.

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2}]
: Equivalent to the cocycle condition (
4.13
), using that
V^{\prime}
is a representation of
\mathfrak{h}
.

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1}]
: Equivalent to cocycle condition (
4.12
), using the
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-equivariance of
\kappa
.

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2}]
: Equivalent to cocycle condition (
4.11
).

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2}]
: This identity has a
V^{\prime}
-component, which is equivalent to the cocycle condition (
4.10
), and an
\mathfrak{h}
-component which is quadratic in the deformation maps

(A\cdot\theta)(v,w)=\delta(\delta(A,v),w)-\delta(\delta(A,w),v)-\delta(A,\alpha(v,w))

(4.15)

for
A\in\mathfrak{h}
and
v,w\in V^{\prime}
.

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1}]
: Equivalent to the second Spencer cocycle condition (
4.9
).

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2}]
: Like the
[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2}]
identity, there is a
V^{\prime}
-component and a
\mathfrak{h}
-component to this Jacobi identity. The former is the first Spencer cocycle condition (
4.8
), while the latter is the quadratic condition

\theta(\kappa_{s},v)+\delta(\gamma(s,s),v)+2\gamma(s,\beta(v,s))=0

(4.16)

for
s\in S^{\prime}
and
v\in V^{\prime}
.

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2}]
: This identity gives the quadratic condition

\theta(v,w)\cdot s+\beta(\alpha(v,w),s)-\beta(v,\beta(w,s))+\beta(w,\beta(v,s))=0,

(4.17)

for
s\in S^{\prime}
,
v,w\in V^{\prime}
.

•

[\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{-2}]
: This has components in
V^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{h}
, both of which are quadratic

\displaystyle\alpha(\alpha(u,v),w)+\theta(u,v)w+\text{cyclic perms.}=0,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\theta(\alpha(u,v),w)+\delta(\theta(u,v),w)+\text{cyclic perms.}=0

(4.19)

for
u,v,w\in V^{\prime}
.

In particular, recalling Lemma
4.6
, we have the following.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}=V^{\prime}\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{s}
be a graded subalgebra, let
\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\delta\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a};\mathfrak{a})
and suppose we have a map
\theta\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}
. Then the brackets (
4.4
) define a filtered deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathfrak{a}
if and only if
\theta
satisfies the equations (
4.15
)–(
4.19
).

Thus the task of determining the possible filtered deformations of a graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{s}
consists of solving the linear equations (
4.8
)–(
4.13
) for
\mu=\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\delta
and then the quadratic equations (
4.15
)–(
4.19
) for
\theta
. We can interpret a
(2,2)
-cocycle
\mu
as an
infinitesimal filtered deformation
of
\mathfrak{a}
, and this deformation
integrates
to an actual filtered deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathfrak{a}
if and only if there exists a solution
\theta
to the equations (
4.15
)–(
4.19
). These equations are highly non-trivial in general, and even if an infinitesimal deformation integrates, the full deformation need not be unique. It is also difficult to give a homological characterisation of when two pairs
(\mu,\theta)
and
(\mu^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
give rise to isomorphic deformations.

4.3
Homogeneity and high supersymmetry

We can get much better homological control over the problem of determining filtered deformations by passing to a particular class of graded subalgebras
\mathfrak{a}
. We have already seen in Lemmas
4.1
and
4.6
that taking
V=V^{\prime}
gives us significant simplifications. We would now like to guarantee that
S^{\prime}
is sufficiently large so that
V=\commutator{S'}{S'}
, which will allow us to fully characterise filtered deformations of
\mathfrak{a}
in terms of Spencer cohomology. To do this, we will make additional assumptions which allow us to use the homogeneity theorem, but we note that the essential ingredient for the results of this section is the fact that
V=\commutator{S'}{S'}
.

4.3.1
The homogeneity theorem

We say that a symmetric Dirac current
\kappa
is
causal
if
\kappa_{s}=\kappa(s,s)
is either timelike or null for all
{s\in S}
. In Lorentzian signature, a symmetric, causal Dirac current on the real pinor module
\mathsf{S}
always exists in any dimension and can be used to construct Dirac currents on extended spinor modules. The existence of symmetric currents can be deduced from the Alekseevsky–Cortés classification
[
2
]
; that they can be chosen to be causal must be shown case-by-case
[
42
]
. These can be used to build symmetric Dirac currents on (
N
-extended) spinor modules
S
by restriction and tensoring with bilinears on an auxiliary module. However such a map is constructed, we have the following.

Theorem 4.8

(homogeneity theorem
[
28
,
42
]
)
.

Let
(V,\eta)
be a Lorentzian vector space of dimension
\dim V>2
and
S
a
(
possibly
N
-extended
)
spinor module of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
with a symmetric, causal Dirac current
\kappa\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S\to V
. If
S^{\prime}
is a vector subspace of
S
with
\dim S^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
, then
{\kappa|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}}
is surjective onto
V
.

For all
A\in\mathfrak{so}(V)
and
s\in S
,
A\cdot s=0
implies that
A\kappa_{s}=0
by
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-equivariance of the Dirac current. Since
\mathfrak{so}(V)
acts faithfully on
V
, the homogeneity theorem has the following corollary.

Corollary 4.9
.

If the Dirac current
\kappa
is causal and
\dim S^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
, then the annihilator of
S^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{so}(V)
is trivial. In particular, any subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
which preserves
S^{\prime}
acts faithfully on
S^{\prime}
.

Guided by the homogeneity theorem (and by physics), let us now fix
\mathfrak{s}
to be the Poincaré superalgebra associated to a Lorentzian inner product space
(V,\eta)
and spinor module
S
with symmetric causal Dirac current
\kappa
. Note that for such algebras we have
\commutator{S'}{S'}=V
for
\dim S^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
, which prompts the following definition.

Definition 4.10
.

A graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}=V\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{s}
with
\dim S^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
is said to be
highly supersymmetric
, and it is
maximally supersymmetric
if
S^{\prime}=S
.

A filtered subdeformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathfrak{s}
is said to be
highly supersymmetric
if
\dim\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\overline{1}}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
, and it is
maximally supersymmetric
if
\dim\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\overline{1}}=\dim S
.

Clearly a filtered subdeformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is highly (maximally) supersymmetric if and only if its associated graded superalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
is highly (maximally) supersymmetric as a graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
.

Remark 4.11
.

The significance of the homogeneity theorem and the assumption of high supersymmetry is that they ensure that
\commutator{S'}{S'}=V
, or
\commutator{\fa_{-1}}{\fa_{-1}}=\mathfrak{s}_{-2}
. If one wishes to relax the assumptions which were made on the signature and Dirac current in order to invoke the theorem, one can simply impose this restriction on the graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
instead of the assumption of high supersymmetry and recover the results of the remainder of Sections
4
and
5
. One might term this
sufficiently supersymmetry
.

4.3.2
Homological properties

Recall Definition
2.4
of some homological properties of graded Lie superalgebras. We now show that highly supersymmetric graded subalgebras of
\mathfrak{s}
satisfy some of these.

Lemma 4.12

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}
be a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
. Then

•

\mathfrak{a}
is fundamental and transitive,

•

\mathfrak{a}
is a full prolongation of degree
2
,

•

\mathsf{H}^{d,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0
for all even
d>2
.

Proof.

Theorem
4.8
precisely says that
\mathfrak{a}
is fundamental, and transitivity follows from faithfulness of the action of
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{a}_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
on
V=\mathfrak{a}_{-2}
or
S^{\prime}=\mathfrak{a}_{-1}
. Corollary
4.2
then says that
{\mathsf{H}^{2,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0}
. For
d>2
,
\mathsf{H}^{d,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0
trivially since
\mathsf{C}^{d,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0
. The beginning of the degree-4 Spencer complex is

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V,\mathfrak{h}\bigr)\longrightarrow\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{3}V,V\bigr)\oplus\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\otimes S^{\prime},S^{\prime}\bigr)\oplus\Hom\bigl(V\otimes\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}\bigr)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\cdots,

and for
\theta\in\mathsf{C}^{4,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V,\mathfrak{h}\bigr)
, we have

\partial\theta(v,s,s)=\theta(v,\kappa_{s})

for all
v\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
, so again by homogeneity,
\partial\theta=0
if and only if
\theta=0
. This shows that
\mathsf{H}^{d,4}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0
, and we have
{\mathsf{H}^{d,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0}
for all
d>4
since
\mathsf{C}^{d,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=0
.
∎

The following pivotal observation is a straightforward generalisation to all spacetime dimensions of
[
32
, Proposition 6]
and also of the analogous results for
maximally
supersymmetric graded subalgebras (those for which
\mathfrak{a}_{-1}=S
) in
[
22
,
33
]
. For the proof, we recall Propositions
2.3
and
2.5
which characterise the defining sequences of filtered deformations in terms of Spencer cohomology and tell us how much freedom we have to redefine them.

Proposition 4.13

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}=V^{\prime}\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
be a graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
a filtered deformation of
\mathfrak{a}
with defining sequence
(\mu,\theta,0,\dots)
as above. Then

(1)

\mu|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}
is a cocycle in
\mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
, and

[\mu|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})^{\mathfrak{h}}
.
Furthermore,
\mu
is a cocycle in
\mathsf{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{a};\mathfrak{a})
.

(2)

If
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is highly supersymmetric and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
is another filtered deformation of
\mathfrak{a}
with degree-
2
deformation map
\mu^{\prime}
such that
[\mu^{\prime}|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}]=[\mu|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}]
then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\cong\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
as filtered Lie superalgebras.

Proof.

The first statement follows by Proposition
2.3
. For the second, if
[\mu^{\prime}|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}]=[\mu|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}]
then
(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}
is a Spencer coboundary, so by point

(
1
)

of Proposition
2.5
there is a new defining sequence
\{\mu^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime},0,\dots\}
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
such that
\mu^{\prime\prime}|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}=\mu|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}
. Then, since
\mathfrak{a}
is a full prolongation of degree 2 by Lemma
4.12
, by point

(
3
)

of Proposition
2.5
we can again find a new defining sequence
\{\mu^{\prime\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime\prime},0,\dots\}
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
such that
\mu^{\prime\prime\prime}=\mu
and
\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}=\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}=\theta|_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}=\theta
.
∎

Note that we already saw the first statement of the proposition above in Section
4.2
. The second statement allows us to strengthen Proposition
4.7
; by Lemma
4.6
, a choice of
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant
\mu_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}
determines
\mu
for
V=V^{\prime}
; we now see that the cohomology class
[\mu_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}]
determines the entire deformation up to isomorphism in the highly supersymmetric case.

What this means in practice is that in order to find the infinitesimal deformations of a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
, we compute
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
and its
\mathfrak{h}
-invariants. The other deforming maps
\delta
and
\theta
will then be determined by the Jacobi identities. Note, though, that the Jacobi identities which are quadratic in the deformation maps may still obstruct the “integration” of the infinitesimal deformation
[\mu_{\mathfrak{a}_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{-}}]
to a filtered deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathfrak{a}
.

4.3.3
Reparametrising the deformation

It will be useful for us to relate
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
to some other cohomology groups. The inclusion
i\colon\mathfrak{a}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{s}
of a graded subalgebra into
\mathfrak{s}
induces the following maps of cochains

\displaystyle i_{*}\colon\ \mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})\to\mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s}),\qquad\phi\mapsto i_{*}\phi=i\circ\phi,

\displaystyle i^{*}\colon\ \mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\to\mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s}),\qquad\phi\mapsto i^{*}\phi=\phi\circ i,

where
\mathfrak{s}
is viewed as representation of
\mathfrak{a}_{-}
via the restriction of the adjoint representation, and the cochain complex
\mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
has been given a grading in the same manner as the Spencer complex (equation (
2.4
)); the differential respects this grading and the maps
i_{*}
,
i^{*}
respect both this grading and the homological grading – that is, they commute with the differential
\partial
. Thus these maps in turn induce maps in cohomology also denoted
i_{*}
and
i^{*}
.

Let us consider these maps in degree
(2,2)
for the highly supersymmetric case. Recall that, by Lemma
4.4
,
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
where the latter is the space of normalised Spencer
(2,2)
-cocycles of
\mathfrak{s}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}
be a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
. Then we have a diagram with exact rows and columns

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathcal{H}^{2,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}

where

\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-}):=\big\{\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}\mid\beta|_{V\otimes S^{\prime}}=0\big\}\cong\ker i^{*}
.

Proof.

For
\beta\colon V\otimes S\to S
,
\gamma\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
such that
\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
, we have
i^{*}[\beta+\gamma]=0
if and only if
\beta|_{V\otimes S^{\prime}}+\gamma|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}=\partial\lambda
, where
\lambda\colon V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
. This implies that
\partial\lambda|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V}=0
, so
\lambda=0
by Lemma
4.1
, thus
\beta|_{V\otimes S^{\prime}}=0
and
\gamma|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}=0
. Note that
\beta|_{V\otimes S^{\prime}}=0
implies
\gamma|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}=0
by the cocycle condition for
\beta+\gamma
, so the isomorphism
\mathcal{H}^{2,2}\cong\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
restricts to give
\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})\cong\ker i^{*}
.

For
\alpha\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to V
,
\beta\colon V\otimes S^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
,
\gamma\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{h}
with
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
,
i_{*}[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=0
if and only if
i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=\partial\lambda
where
\lambda:V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
. Then
\gamma(s,s)=-\lambda(\kappa(s,s))
for all
s\in S^{\prime}
; in particular,
\lambda(V)=\lambda(\commutator{S'}{S'})\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
, or equivalently
\lambda=i_{*}\lambda^{\prime}
for some
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}^{2,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=\Hom(V,\mathfrak{h})
. It follows that
\alpha+\beta+\gamma=\partial\lambda^{\prime}
, so
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=0
, showing that
i_{*}\colon\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})\to\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
is injective.
∎

By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
, in the highly supersymmetric case any cohomology class in
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
has a unique representative in the space of normalised cocycles

\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})=\bigl\{\beta+\gamma\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\mid\beta\in\Hom(V\otimes S^{\prime},S),\,\gamma\in\Hom\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime},\mathfrak{so}(V)\bigr)\bigr\},

and we have a splitting of
\mathfrak{h}
-modules
\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})=\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})\oplus\mathsf{B}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
, whence as
\mathfrak{h}
-modules
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
. We thus have an exact sequence at the level of cocycles corresponding to the bottom row of the diagram in the lemma above

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{2,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}

On the other hand, any cohomology class in
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
defines a unique normalised cocycle in
\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
by pushing forward to
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
and then identifying the normalised cocycle. Since
i_{*}
is injective in cohomology, if a cocycle in
\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
is obtained from a class in
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
, that class is unique. We can be much more explicit about this: if
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
then there exists a unique element
\lambda\in\mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})=\Hom(V,\mathfrak{so}(V))
such that

\alpha(v,w)=\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v;

(4.20)

the components
\widetilde{\beta}\colon V\otimes S^{\prime}\to S
and
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
of the unique normalised cocycle
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
such that
i_{*}[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=[\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}]
are then given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)=\beta(v,s)-\lambda(v)\cdot s,\qquad\widetilde{\gamma}(s,s)=\gamma(s,s)+\lambda(\kappa_{s}),

(4.21)

for
v\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
. The maps
\lambda
,
\widetilde{\beta}
,
\widetilde{\gamma}
are unique for a given cocycle
\alpha+\beta+\gamma
, while a different choice of representative for the same cohomology class corresponds to shifting
\lambda
by a map
V\to\mathfrak{h}
. We note that

i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}+\partial\lambda
,
and that we have the constraints

\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s\in S^{\prime}
,
\widetilde{\gamma}(s,s)-\lambda(\kappa_{s})\in\mathfrak{h}

for all
v\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
. Finally, observe that
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant if and only if
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
is (which is the case if and only if
\widetilde{\beta}
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant). If this is the case, the discussion following Lemma
4.6
tells us that the map
\delta\colon\mathfrak{h}\otimes V\to\mathfrak{h}
defined by

\delta(A,v)=\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(Av)

(4.22)

is the unique such map so that
\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\delta\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a};\mathfrak{a})
.

The discussion above allows us to reparametrise the deformed brackets (
4.4
) in the highly supersymmetric case.

Proposition 4.15
.

If
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is a highly supersymmetric filtered deformation of a graded subalgebra
{\mathfrak{a}=V\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}}
of
\mathfrak{s}
, then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
has a presentation of the form

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{B}=\underbrace{AB-BA}_{\mathfrak{h}},\qquad\commutator{s}{s}=\underbrace{\kappa_{s}}_{V}+\underbrace{\widetilde{\gamma}(s,s)-\lambda(\kappa_{s})}_{\mathfrak{h}},

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{v}=\underbrace{Av}_{V}+\underbrace{\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(Av)}_{\mathfrak{h}},\qquad\commutator{v}{s}=\underbrace{\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s}_{S^{\prime}},\qquad\commutator{A}{s}=\underbrace{A\cdot s}_{S^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\commutator{v}{w}=\underbrace{\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v}_{V}+\underbrace{\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)-\lambda(\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v)+\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\lambda(w)}}_{\mathfrak{h}}

(4.23)

for
A,B\in\mathfrak{h}
,
v,w\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
, where the deforming maps are

\widetilde{\beta}\colon\ V\otimes S^{\prime}\to S,\qquad\widetilde{\gamma}\colon\ \mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{so}(V),\qquad\lambda\colon\ V\to\mathfrak{so}(V),\qquad\widetilde{\theta}\colon\ \mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V),

where
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})^{\mathfrak{h}}
,
\widetilde{\theta}
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant and the remaining Jacobi identities are equivalent to

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(\kappa_{s},v)+2\widetilde{\gamma}\bigl(s,\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s\bigr)-\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\gammatilde(s,s)}=0,

(4.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\cdot s=\widetilde{\beta}\bigl(v,\widetilde{\beta}(w,s)+\lambda(w)\cdot s\bigr)-\lambda(w)\cdot\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\widetilde{\beta}(\lambda(w)v,s)

\displaystyle\phantom{\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\cdot s=}{}-\widetilde{\beta}\bigl(w,\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s\bigr)+\lambda(v)\cdot\widetilde{\beta}(w,s)-\widetilde{\beta}(\lambda(v)w,s),

(4.25)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(u,v)w+\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)u+\widetilde{\theta}(w,u)v=0,

(4.26)

\displaystyle\bigl(\lambda(u)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(v,w)+\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(w,u)+\bigl(\lambda(w)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(u,v)=0

(4.27)

for all
u,v,w\in V
and
s\in S^{\prime}
.
Two deformations
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
described in this way are isomorphic as filtered Lie superalgebras if and only if they determine the same element
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})^{\mathfrak{h}}
.

Proof.

We begin with a presentation of the form (
4.4
), so that
\alpha+\beta+\gamma
is a
(2,2)
-Spencer cocycle for
\mathfrak{a}
with
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant cohomology class and then define
\lambda,\widetilde{\beta},\widetilde{\gamma}
by equations (
4.20
)–(
4.21
);
\delta
must then be given by (
4.22
). The brackets then take the claimed form if we define

\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)=\theta(v,w)+\lambda(\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v)-\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\lambda(w)}
.
It remains to check the Jacobi identities (
4.15
)–(
4.19
) in our new variables. Substituting the definition of
\delta
into the first identity (
4.15
) gives us
A\cdot\widetilde{\theta}=0
for all
A\in\mathfrak{h}
. Next, we find that (
4.16
) implies (
4.24
)

\displaystyle\theta(\kappa_{s},v)+\delta(\gamma(s,s),v)+2\gamma(s,\beta(v,s))

\displaystyle\qquad=\theta(\kappa_{s},v)+\commutator{\gammatilde(s,s)}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(\widetilde{\gamma}(s,s)v)-\commutator{\lambda(\kappa_s)}{\lambda(v)}+\lambda(\lambda(\kappa_{s})v)

\displaystyle\phantom{\qquad=}{}+2\widetilde{\gamma}(s,\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s)-2\lambda(\kappa(s,\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s))

\displaystyle\qquad=\theta(\kappa_{s},v)+\lambda\quantity(\lambda(\kappa_s)v - 2\kappa(s,\lambda(v)\cdot s))-\commutator{\lambda(\kappa_s)}{\lambda(v)}+2\widetilde{\gamma}(s,\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s)

\displaystyle\phantom{\qquad=}{}-\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\gammatilde(s,s)}-\lambda(\kappa(s,s)v+2\kappa(s,\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)))

\displaystyle\qquad=\widetilde{\theta}(\kappa_{s},v)+2\widetilde{\gamma}(s,\widetilde{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s)-\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\gammatilde(s,s)},

where in the last line we have used the definition of
\widetilde{\theta}
, a cocycle condition for
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}
and
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-invariance of
\kappa
. Substitution of the change of variables followed by some trivial rearrangement shows that (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) are equivalent to (
4.25
) and (
4.26
) respectively. Finally, (
4.19
) gives
\bigl(\lambda(u)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(v,w)+\lambda\bigl(\widetilde{\theta}(u,v)w\bigr)+\text{cyclic perms.}=0
,
but then substitution of (
4.26
) yields (
4.27
). The final claim follows by Proposition
4.13
since the cocycle
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})^{\mathfrak{h}}
is uniquely determined by the cohomology class
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
.
∎

4.4
Realisable subdeformations

We have just seen that if
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
is the Spencer cocycle associated to some presentation of a filtered deformation of a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{s}
, then there is an
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant cocycle
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
such that
i_{*}[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=[\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}]
. We will now restrict our attention to cocycles obeying a stronger version of this and the corresponding filtered subdeformations of
\mathfrak{s}
(where they exist). Essentially, we demand that
\widetilde{\beta}+\widetilde{\gamma}
be the pull-back of some
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant element
\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
.

4.4.1
Admissibility

Definition 4.16
.

A cohomology class
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
for a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
of
\mathfrak{s}
is
admissible
if we have

i_{*}[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=i^{*}\big[\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\big]

in
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
for some
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant cocycle
\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
.

A cocycle in
\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
is
admissible
if its cohomology class is admissible.

Note that an admissible cohomology class is in particular
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant. Unpacking the definition a little, for admissible cocycles we have

i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=i^{*}(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma})+\partial\lambda

(4.28)

for a unique
\lambda\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})=\Hom(V,\mathfrak{so}(V))
and
\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\in\bigl(\mathcal{H}^{2,2}\bigr)^{\mathfrak{h}}
determined uniquely up to elements in
\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
. If we fix an admissible cohomology class but not a particular representative,
\lambda
is only determined up to elements in
\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})=\Hom(V,\mathfrak{h})
.

Recalling that a highly supersymmetric subdeformation of
\mathfrak{s}
uniquely determines an element of
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})^{\mathfrak{h}}
by Proposition
4.13
, we make the following definition.

Definition 4.17

(geometric realisability)
.

A highly supersymmetric filtered subdeformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathfrak{s}
is
(
geometrically
)
realisable
if the associated cohomology class in
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})^{\mathfrak{h}}
is admissible.

The definitions above are inspired by, although
not
equivalent to
[
32
, Definition 9]
in the context of 11-dimensional supergravity. There, one finds that
\mathcal{H}^{2,2}\cong\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{4}V
, and it is the 4-form
\varphi
associated to
\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}
which is called admissible if the conditions above are met
and
an additional algebraic condition related to the fact that
\varphi
should considered to be the value at a point of a closed 4-form field strength on some supergravity background is imposed. Our definition here is weaker because it is not clear
a priori
how this condition should be generalised.

Let us make some particular remarks about these definitions in the maximally supersymmetric case, where
\mathfrak{a}_{-}=\mathfrak{s}_{-}
. Here, the map
i^{*}\colon\mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\to\mathsf{C}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
whose induced map in cohomology appears in the definition of admissibility, is the identity map. An admissible cocycle for
\mathfrak{a}
is then simply a cocycle in
\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
whose cohomology class is
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
-invariant. It follows that
all
maximally supersymmetric filtered deformations of
\mathfrak{s}
are realisable.

We will justify the term “geometrically realisable” in Section
5.2
(see Theorem
5.6
). Our goal in this section will be to determine the conditions under which an admissible cohomology class of a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{s}
is
integrable
, in the sense that there exists a corresponding geometrically realisable filtered deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
. We first record the following. Note that the homogeneity theorem (see Theorem
4.8
) is required for this result as well as for many in Section
4.4.2
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\alpha+\beta+\gamma
be an admissible cocycle parametrised as in (
4.28
). Then

•

for all
v\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
,
\widehat{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s\in S^{\prime}
;

•

for all
s,s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
,
\widehat{\gamma}(s,s^{\prime})-\lambda(\kappa(s,s^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{h}
;

•

for all
A\in\mathfrak{h}
,
v\in V
,
\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(Av)\in\mathfrak{h}
.

Proof.

The first two claims follow directly from the definition. For the third, by Theorem
4.8
we can take
v=\kappa_{s}
for some
s\in S^{\prime}
. Recalling that the action of
\mathfrak{h}
preserves
S^{\prime}
and using
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-equivariance of
\kappa
and
\mathfrak{h}
-invariance of
\widehat{\gamma}
, we have

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{\lambda(\kappa_s)}-\lambda(A\kappa_{s})

\displaystyle=\commutator{A}{\lambda(\kappa_s)}-2\lambda(\kappa(As,s))

\displaystyle=\commutator{A}{\lambda(\kappa_s)-\gamma(s,s)}+2(\widehat{\gamma}(As,s)-\lambda(\kappa(As,s))),

and both of the terms in the last expression lie in
\mathfrak{h}
by the second claim of the lemma.
∎

4.4.2
Integrability

Suppose we have an admissible cocycle parametrised as in (
4.28
) and take
\widetilde{\beta}=i^{*}\widehat{\beta}
,
\widetilde{\gamma}=i^{*}\widehat{\gamma}
. We wish to know whether it is possible to define a (geometrically realisable) filtered subdeformation as in Proposition
4.15
using these maps in the definition of the bracket (
4.23
). By the above lemma, it remains only to check whether there exists an
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
such that

\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)-\lambda(\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v)+\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\lambda(w)}\in\mathfrak{h}

(4.29)

satisfying the equations (
4.24
) to (
4.27
). We first observe that equations (
4.24
) and (
4.25
) simplify slightly

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(v,\kappa_{s})=2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(v,s)\bigr)-\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)(s,s),

(4.30)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\cdot s=\widehat{\beta}\bigl(v,\widehat{\beta}(w,s)\bigr)-\widehat{\beta}\bigl(w,\widehat{\beta}(v,s)\bigr)+\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\beta}\bigr)(w,s)-\bigl(\lambda(w)\cdot\widehat{\beta}\bigr)(v,s),

(4.31)

and by Theorem
4.8
, either equation fully determines
\widetilde{\theta}
(if it exists). In fact,
\widetilde{\theta}
is uniquely determined by the admissible cohomology class: it depends only on
i^{*}\widehat{\beta}
,
i^{*}\widehat{\gamma}
and
\lambda
, so a shift in
{\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}}
by an element of
\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
does not change it, while by
\mathfrak{h}
-invariance of
\widehat{\beta}
and
\widehat{\gamma}
, changing
\lambda
by addition of a map
V\to\mathfrak{h}
also does not change
\widetilde{\theta}
. There may, however, be obstructions to the existence of
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
even for an admissible cocycle. For now, we will postpone considering this issue and show that a map
\widetilde{\theta}
determined by the equations above automatically satisfies all of the other required properties.

Lemma 4.19
.

Any map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
satisfying equation (
4.30
) also satisfies (
4.29
).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.8
, it is sufficient to show the result for
w=\kappa_{s}
for some
s\in S^{\prime}
; we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(v,\kappa_{s})-\lambda(\lambda(v)\kappa_{s}-\lambda(\kappa_{s})v)+\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\lambda(\kappa_s)}

\displaystyle\qquad=2\big\{\quantity(\gammahat-\lambda\circ\kappa)(s,\widehat{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s)\big\}

\displaystyle\phantom{\qquad=}{}+\quantity{\comm{(\gammahat-\lambda\circ\kappa)(s,s)}{\lambda(v)} - \lambda((\gammahat-\lambda\circ\kappa)(s,s)v)},

and since
(\widehat{\gamma}-\lambda\circ\kappa)|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}
takes values in
\mathfrak{h}
, Lemma
4.18
implies that both of the expressions within the braces lie in
\mathfrak{h}
.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

Any map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
satisfying equation (
4.31
) also satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\kappa_{s}=2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(v,s),s\bigr)w-2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(w,s),s\bigr)v-\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)(s,s)w+\quantity(\lambda(w)\cdot\gammahat)(s,s)v

for all
v,w\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
, and this equation determines
\widetilde{\theta}
uniquely.

Proof.

First, it will be useful to depolarise one of the cocycle conditions for
\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}

2\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(v,s),s^{\prime}\bigr)+2\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(v,s^{\prime}),s\bigr)+2\widehat{\gamma}(s,s^{\prime})v=0.

(4.32)

Using
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-invariance of
\kappa
, equation (
4.31
) gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\kappa_{s}=2\kappa\bigl(\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\cdot s,s\bigr)={}

\displaystyle 2\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(v,\widehat{\beta}(w,s)\bigr),s)+2\kappa\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\beta}(w,s),s\bigr)

\displaystyle-2\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(\lambda(v)w,s),s\bigr)-2\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(w,\lambda(v)\cdot s),s\bigr)-(v\leftrightarrow w).

We now use equation (
4.32
) with
s^{\prime}=s,\lambda(v)\cdot s
and
\widehat{\beta}(w,s)
to replace some of the terms above and then use
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-invariance of
\kappa
again to conclude that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\kappa_{s}={}

\displaystyle-2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(w,s),s\bigr)v-2\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(v,s),\widehat{\beta}(w,s)\bigr)+2\kappa\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\beta}(w,s),s\bigr)

\displaystyle+\widehat{\gamma}(\lambda(v)\cdot s,s)v+\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)(\lambda(v)w)+2\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(w,s),\lambda(v)\cdot s\bigr)-(v\leftrightarrow w)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(v,s),s\bigr)w+\lambda(v)\kappa\bigl(\widehat{\beta}(w,s),s\bigr)

\displaystyle+\gamma(s,s)(\lambda(v)w)+\widehat{\gamma}(\lambda(v)\cdot s,s)w-(v\leftrightarrow w),

which is the desired expression. In the final equality, we have used the skew-symmetry of the expression in
v
and
w
. By Theorem
4.8
, this defines
\widetilde{\theta}
uniquely.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

For any map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
, we have the following:

•

Either equation (
4.30
) or (
4.31
) implies that
\widetilde{\theta}
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant.

•

Assuming equation (
4.30
), equation (
4.20
) is equivalent to the algebraic Bianchi identity (
4.26
). In particular, equations (
4.30
) and (
4.31
) together imply the Bianchi identity.

•

Equations (
4.30
) and (
4.31
) together imply (
4.27
).

Proof.

The first claim follows by some fairly straightforward but long manipulations which use
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-invariance of
\kappa
,
\mathfrak{h}
-invariance of
\widehat{\beta}
and
\widehat{\gamma}
and the fact that
\lambda(Av)-\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}\in\mathfrak{h}
for all
{A\in\mathfrak{h}}
. For the second claim, let us assume equation (
4.30
) holds. Then equation (
4.20
) gives
{\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)\kappa_{s}=\widetilde{\theta}(v,\kappa_{s})w-\widetilde{\theta}(w,\kappa_{s})v}

for all
v,w\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
, which by the homogeneity theorem is the Bianchi identity. Conversely, if we assume the Bianchi identity, the above equation along with (
4.30
) gives us (
4.20
).

For the final claim let us assume that
\widetilde{\theta}
is given by (
4.30
) and note that by Theorem
4.8
we can assume without loss of generality that
w=\kappa_{s}
for arbitrary
s\in S^{\prime}
. Then we can use
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-invariance of
\kappa
to compute

\displaystyle\bigl(\lambda(u)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(v,\kappa_{s})+\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(\kappa_{s},u)={}

\displaystyle\bigl((\lambda(\lambda(u)v-\lambda(v)u)-\commutator{\lambda(u)}{\lambda(v)})\cdot\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)(s,s)

\displaystyle+2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,(\lambda(u)\cdot\widehat{\beta})(v,s)-(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\beta})(u,s)\bigr)

\displaystyle+2(\lambda(u)\cdot\widehat{\gamma})\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(v,s)\bigr)-2(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\gamma})\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(u,s)\bigr).

On the other hand, we recall that
\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)-\lambda(\kappa_{s})\in\mathfrak{h}
, and that
\widetilde{\theta}
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant by the first part of the lemma, whence
\lambda(\kappa_{s})\cdot\widetilde{\theta}=\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}
. Using (
4.30
) and a cocycle condition, we find
17
17

17

Actually, it’s necessarily to depolarise (
4.30
) in order to evaluate expressions like
\widetilde{\theta}(v,\kappa(s,s^{\prime}))
for
v\in V

s,s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\bigl(\lambda(\kappa_{s})\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(u,v)={}

\displaystyle[\widehat{\gamma}(s,s),\widetilde{\theta}(u,w)]+2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(u,\widehat{\beta}(v,s))-\widehat{\beta}(v,\widehat{\beta}(u,s))\bigr)

\displaystyle-2(\lambda(u)\cdot\widehat{\gamma})\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(v,s)\bigr)+2(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\gamma})\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(u,s)\bigr).

Putting the two calculations together and then using (
4.31
), we find

\displaystyle\bigl(\lambda(u)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(v,\kappa_{s})+\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(\kappa_{s},u)+\bigl(\lambda(\kappa_{s})\cdot\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)(u,v)

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl((\lambda(\lambda(u)v-\lambda(v)u)-\commutator{\lambda(u)}{\lambda(v)})\cdot\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)(s,s)-\big[\widetilde{\theta}(u,w),\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)\big]

\displaystyle\phantom{\qquad=}{}+2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}\bigl(u,\widehat{\beta}(v,s)\bigr)-\widehat{\beta}\bigl(v,\widehat{\beta}(u,s)\bigr)\bigr)+2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,\bigl(\lambda(u)\cdot\widehat{\beta}\bigr)(v,s)-\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\beta}\bigr)(u,s)\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad=-\bigl(\bigl(\widetilde{\theta}(u,w)+\commutator{\lambda(u)}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(\lambda(u)v-\lambda(v)u)\bigr)\cdot\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)(s,s).

The final line vanishes by Lemma
4.19
and
\mathfrak{h}
-invariance of
\widehat{\gamma}
.
∎

Thus the problem of integrating an admissible cocycle reduces to the question of whether a map satisfying the equations (
4.30
) and (
4.31
) exists. Let us first consider equation (
4.30
). We would like to interpret this as the definition of a map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
, but
a priori
we only have that

\Theta(v,s,s)=2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(v,s)\bigr)-\bigl(\lambda(v)\cdot\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)(s,s)

(4.33)

defines a map
\Theta\colon V\otimes\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
. So we require that
\Theta
factorises as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{so}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id\otimes\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\theta}}

and that
\widetilde{\theta}
is skew-symmetric. We define the
Dirac kernel
of
S^{\prime}
by
\mathfrak{D}=\ker\kappa|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}
and note that
\Theta
factorises as above if and only if it annihilates the Dirac kernel, i.e.,
\Theta(V\otimes\mathfrak{D})=0
. We then find that
\widetilde{\theta}
is automatically skew-symmetric.

Lemma 4.22
.

Suppose that
\Theta
annihilates the Dirac kernel and thus factors as described above. Then equation (
4.30
) defines a map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
.

Proof.

The factorisation of
\Theta
gives us a map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon V\otimes V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
satisfying (
4.30
). To show that
\widetilde{\theta}
is alternating, it is sufficient to show that
\widetilde{\theta}(\kappa_{s},\kappa_{s})=0
for all
s\in S^{\prime}
. Using the fact that
