\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)-\lambda(\kappa_{s})\in\mathfrak{h}
for
s\in S^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{h}
-invariance of
\widehat{\gamma}
, we have
\lambda(\kappa_{s})\cdot\widehat{\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)\cdot\widehat{\gamma}
, so

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(\kappa_{s},\kappa_{s})

\displaystyle=2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(\kappa_{s},s)\bigr)-\bigl(\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)\cdot\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)(s,s)

\displaystyle=2\widehat{\gamma}\bigl(s,\widehat{\beta}(\kappa_{s},s)+\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)\cdot s\bigr)-\commutator{\gammahat(s,s)}{\gammahat(s,s)}=0,

where we have used a cocycle condition in the last equality.
∎

Definition 4.23
.

Let
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]
be an admissible cohomology class and, recalling that it does not depend on the choice of cocycle representative, let
\Theta\colon V\otimes\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
be the map defined by equation (
4.33
). We say that
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]
is
integrable
if

(1)

\Theta
annihilates the Dirac kernel
\mathfrak{D}
of
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}
,

(2)

the map
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
defined by
\widetilde{\theta}\circ\kappa=\Theta
satisfies (
4.31
).

An admissible cocycle is
integrable
if its cohomology class is.

By construction, we have the following refinement of Proposition
4.15
.

Theorem 4.24

(integration of admissible cocycles)
.

Let
\alpha+\beta+\gamma
be an admissible, integrable Spencer
(2,2)
-cocycle for a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
of
\mathfrak{s}
with
i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=i^{*}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)+\partial\lambda
and let
\widetilde{\theta}\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
be the map defined by equation (
4.30
). Then the brackets

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{B}=\underbrace{AB-BA}_{\mathfrak{h}},\qquad\commutator{s}{s}=\underbrace{\kappa_{s}}_{V}+\underbrace{\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)-\lambda(\kappa_{s})}_{\mathfrak{h}},

\displaystyle\commutator{A}{v}=\underbrace{Av}_{V}+\underbrace{\commutator{A}{\lambda(v)}-\lambda(Av)}_{\mathfrak{h}},\qquad\commutator{v}{s}=\underbrace{\widehat{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s}_{S^{\prime}},\qquad\commutator{A}{s}=\underbrace{A\cdot s}_{S^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\commutator{v}{w}=\underbrace{\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v}_{V}+\underbrace{\widetilde{\theta}(v,w)-\lambda(\lambda(v)w-\lambda(w)v)+\commutator{\lambda(v)}{\lambda(w)}}_{\mathfrak{h}}

define a geometrically realisable filtered deformation of
\mathfrak{a}
. Two such deformations
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
are isomorphic if and only if
\bigl(\widehat{\beta}^{\prime}+\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\bigr)-\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)\in\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})
.

Where
\mathfrak{a}
is a
maximally
supersymmetric graded subalgebra of the (minimal) Poincaré superalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
in 4, 5, 6 or 11 spacetime dimensions, there is no obstruction to an admissible cocycle being integrable
[
10
,
22
,
23
,
33
]
. We already observed that admissible cohomology classes are simply elements of
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{a})^{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}
if
\mathfrak{a}
is highly supersymmetric, so
any
such cohomology class defines a realisable filtered deformation in these cases. The question of whether obstructions exist in the highly supersymmetric (but sub-maximal) case is still open even in these cases.

4.5
The classification problem for highly supersymmetric

odd-generated filtered subdeformations

We will now outline a scheme for the classification of highly supersymmetric odd-generated realisable filtered subdeformations
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathfrak{s}
. Odd-generated here means that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is generated by its odd subspace;
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\overline{0}}=\commutator{\fatilde_{\overline{1}}}{\fatilde_{\overline{1}}}
. We note that this is very close to a classification of
all
highly supersymmetric realisable filtered subdeformations, since if
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is a general such subdeformation, the ideal subalgebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}=\commutator{\fatilde_{\overline{1}}}{\fatilde_{\overline{1}}}\oplus\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{\overline{1}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
(which we will call the
odd-generated ideal
) is odd-generated realisable, and for the associated graded subalgebras of
\mathfrak{s}
we have
\mathfrak{a}_{-2}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{a}_{-2}
,
{\mathfrak{a}_{-1}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{a}_{-1}}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}_{0}
. Thus only the zero-graded part is not determined by the odd-generated subalgebra, and by inspection of (
4.24
), it is clear that the deformation maps for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
are also fully determined by those of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
. Thus the only data which distinguishes
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
from its odd-generated ideal is the choice of some subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
which contains
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\prime}
and preserves both
S^{\prime}
and
{\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}}
.

We generalise the results of
[
32
, Section 4]
and
[
56
, Section 5]
which treat the 11-dimensional case.

4.5.1
Isomorphisms and embeddings

We first generalise Definitions 5 and 11 from
[
32
]
. For this, consider
\mathfrak{s}=V\oplus S\oplus\mathfrak{so}(V)
under the action of its graded automorphism group

\GrAut\mathfrak{s}=\quantity{ g\in\GL(\fs)\mid g\cdot\fs_i = \fs_i \text{ and } \forall X,Y\in\fs, g\cdot\comm{X}{Y}=\comm{g\cdot X}{g\cdot Y}}.

We note that there is a natural action of
\Spin_{0}(V)
as “inner” graded automorphisms, in the sense that this group integrates the Lie algebra of inner graded derivations induced by the action of
\mathfrak{s}_{0}=\mathfrak{so}(V)
, and this gives us an embedding
\Spin_{0}(V)\hookrightarrow\GrAut\mathfrak{s}
.
If the Dirac current (which by definition is
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-equivariant) is equivariant under the whole spin group, then this inner automorphism group can be enlarged to
\Spin(V)
. The group of graded automorphisms also contains a one-dimensional subgroup corresponding to dilatations and may additionally contain an “
R
-symmetry” group (for a recent review and classification see
[
35
]
.

The role played by
\GrAut\mathfrak{s}
in our formalism is played by
\Spin(V)
in
[
32
]
, which is a somewhat more natural choice in that more concrete context. We note that with the choices made in loc. cit.,
\GrAut\mathfrak{s}
contains the whole spin group, while the
R
-symmetry group is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and embeds into
\GrAut\mathfrak{s}
as the centre of the isomorphic image of
\Spin(V)
. In this case,
\Gr\Aut\mathfrak{s}\cong\Spin(V)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where the latter factor is a multiplicative group acting as dilatations.

Note that if
\mathfrak{a}
is a graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
then
g\cdot\mathfrak{a}
is also a graded subalgebra for all
g\in G
, with left multiplication by
g
defining a graded isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}\cong g\cdot\mathfrak{a}
.

Definition 4.25
.

Two filtered subdeformations
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
of the Poincaré superalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
are
isomorphic
if there exists a (strict) filtered isomorphism of Lie superalgebras
\Phi\colon\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
such that the associated graded morphism
\Gr\Phi\colon\mathfrak{a}\to\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
is given for all
x\in\mathfrak{a}
, by
\Gr\Phi(x)=g\cdot x
for some
{g\in\GrAut\mathfrak{s}}
.

An
embedding
of subdeformations
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{s}
is an injective (strict) filtered morphism of Lie superalgebras which is an isomorphism of subdeformations onto its image.

Note that two filtered subdeformations of
\mathfrak{s}
being isomorphic
as subdeformations
is stronger than them simply being isomorphic as filtered Lie superalgebras.

With this in mind, we can strengthen point (2) of Proposition
4.13
by noting that two highly supersymmetric deformations of the same subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{s}
inducing the same cohomology class in
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
are isomorphic
as subdeformations
and not just as filtered Lie superalgebras. Indeed, this follows immediately from the proof of the proposition, which also shows in particular that the associated graded automorphism of
\mathfrak{a}
is the identity map.

4.5.2
Kernels and sections of the Dirac current

Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
with
\dim S^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
and recall that by Theorem
4.8
,
\kappa|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}
is surjective, so we have a short exact sequence of vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}}

where
\mathfrak{D}=\ker\kappa|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}
is the Dirac kernel of
S^{\prime}
as in Section
4.4.2
. If
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
is a subalgebra preserving
S^{\prime}
, then this is a sequence of
\mathfrak{h}
-modules. We define a
section of the Dirac current
\kappa
(understood to mean
\kappa
restricted to
S^{\prime}
) as a linear map
\Sigma\colon V\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}
which splits the above as a sequence of vector spaces, i.e.,
\kappa\circ\Sigma=\Id_{V}
, giving a non-canonical decomposition of vector spaces (not of
\mathfrak{h}
-modules)
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\simeq V\oplus\mathfrak{D}
. Two such sections differ by a map
V\to\mathfrak{D}
.

In the following, we recall from Section
4.3.3
the maps of cochains
i_{*}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})\to\!\mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
,
\phi\mapsto i\circ\phi
and
i^{*}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{s}_{-};\mathfrak{s})\to\mathsf{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
,
\phi\mapsto\phi\circ i
(and the induced maps of cochains and cohomology classes) induced by the inclusion
i\colon\mathfrak{a}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{s}
.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}=V\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
be a highly supersymmetric graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
and let
\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
. Then there exists a cohomology class
[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
such that
i_{*}[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=i^{*}[\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}]
if and only if

(1)

\widehat{\gamma}(\mathfrak{D})\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
,

(2)

for all
v\in V
,
\bigl(\imath_{v}\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(v))\bigr)(S^{\prime})\subseteq S^{\prime}
for some
(
hence any
)
section
\Sigma
of
\kappa
.

Moreover, if such a cohomology class exists, for a fixed section
\Sigma
there exists a unique representative
\alpha+\beta+\gamma
such that
i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=i^{*}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)+\partial\bigl(\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma\bigr)
.

Proof.

Suppose
i_{*}[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=i^{*}\big[\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\big]
, so that
i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=i^{*}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)+\partial\lambda
for some unique
\lambda\in\mathsf{C}^{2,1}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})=\Hom(V,\mathfrak{so}(V))
. On one hand, restricting to
\mathfrak{D}\subseteq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}
, we find that
{\widehat{\gamma}|_{\mathfrak{D}}=\gamma|_{\mathfrak{D}}}
, which takes values in
\mathfrak{h}
. Now let
\Sigma\colon V\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}
be a section of
\kappa
and note that we have

\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma-\lambda=\quantity(\gammahat- \lambda\circ\kappa)\circ\Sigma=\gamma\circ\Sigma

which also takes values in
\mathfrak{h}
. On the other hand, restricting to
V\otimes S^{\prime}
, for all
v\in V
and
s\in S^{\prime}
we have
\widehat{\beta}(v,s)+\lambda(v)\cdot s=\beta(v,s)\in S^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\imath_{v}\widehat{\beta}+\lambda(v)
preserves
S^{\prime}
, but then

\imath_{v}\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(v))=(\imath_{v}\widehat{\beta}+\lambda(v))+(\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma-\lambda)(v)

also preserves
S^{\prime}
since the action of
\mathfrak{h}
preserves
S^{\prime}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\widehat{\gamma}(\mathfrak{D})\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
and
\imath_{v}\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(v))
preserves
S^{\prime}
for all
v\in V
for some (hence any
18
18

18

Since any two sections differ by a map
V\to\mathfrak{D}
, the conditions together imply that
\imath_{v}\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma^{\prime}(v))
preserves
S^{\prime}
for any other section
\Sigma^{\prime}
as well.
)
section
\Sigma
. Then define
\lambda\colon V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
by
\lambda=\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma
, giving us
(\widehat{\gamma}-\lambda\circ\kappa)(\mathfrak{D})=\widehat{\gamma}(\mathfrak{D})\in\mathfrak{h}
and
(\widehat{\gamma}-\lambda\circ\kappa)\circ\Sigma=\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma-\lambda=0
which together imply that
(\widehat{\gamma}-\lambda\circ\kappa)\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\bigr)\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
. Thus we can define a cocycle
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\in\mathsf{Z}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{a})
with
i_{*}[\alpha+\beta+\gamma]=i^{*}\big[\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\big]
by

\displaystyle\alpha(v,w)=\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(v))w-\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(w))(v),\qquad\beta(v,s)=\widehat{\beta}(v,s)+\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(v))\cdot s,

\displaystyle\gamma(s,s)=\widehat{\gamma}(s,s)-\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(\kappa_{s})),

for
v,w\in V
,
s\in S^{\prime}
. This also shows the final claim.
∎

Note that in the lemma above, a change of section
\Sigma
does not change the cohomology class of a cocycle
i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=i^{*}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)+\partial\bigl(\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma\bigr)
. Indeed, if
\Sigma^{\prime}
is another section, then

i^{*}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)+\partial(\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma^{\prime})-\bigl(i^{*}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)+\partial\bigl(\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma\bigr)\bigr)=\partial(\widehat{\gamma}\circ(\Sigma^{\prime}-\Sigma))

and since
(\Sigma^{\prime}-\Sigma)
takes values in
\mathfrak{D}
,
\widehat{\gamma}\circ(\Sigma^{\prime}-\Sigma)
takes values in
\mathfrak{h}
.

4.5.3
Lie pairs, envelopes and odd-generated realisable subeformations

We now introduce some technology for building subdeformations from normalised cocycles. Note that since all admissible cocycles will now be discussed only in terms of a fixed element of
\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
, we no longer decorate the components of such elements with hats, trusting that this will not cause confusion.

Definition 4.27

(envelopes and Lie pairs)
.

Fix a subspace
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
with
\dim S^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
and
{\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}}
. We define the
envelope of
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
as the subspace

\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}=\gamma(\mathfrak{D})\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V)
.
We say that
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
is a
Lie pair
if for all
A\in\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}

(1)

A\cdot\beta=0
,

(2)

A\cdot S^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
.

Our use of the term “Lie pair” follows our sources
[
32
,
56
]
and is motivated by the first part of the following result, reconciling it with the Lie pairs defined in the appendices (see Definition
A.3
).

Proposition 4.28
.

If
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
is a Lie pair, then
\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
. If additionally
\imath_{v}\beta+\gamma(\Sigma(v))
preserves
S^{\prime}
for some section
\Sigma
of
\kappa
, then
i^{*}(\beta+\gamma)+\partial(\gamma\circ\Sigma)
defines an admissible cocycle for the graded subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}=V\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
of
\mathfrak{s}
. Furthermore, if this cocycle is integrable, the associated realisable deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
is odd-generated.

Conversely, for any odd-generated realisable filtered subdeformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathfrak{s}
there exists a Lie pair
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
such that
\Gr\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\cong\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
.

Proof.

Let
A,B\in\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
. Then there exists
\omega\in\mathfrak{D}
such that
B=\gamma(\omega)
; note that
A\cdot\beta=0\implies A\cdot\gamma=0
, so
\commutator{A}{B}=\commutator{A}{\gamma(\omega)}=\gamma(A\cdot\omega)
. On the other hand, the action of
A
preserves
S^{\prime}
, whence
A\cdot\omega\in\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}
, and
\kappa(A\omega)=A\kappa(\omega)=0
, so
A\cdot\omega\in\mathfrak{D}
. Thus
\commutator{A}{B}\in\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
, so the latter is indeed a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
. Since the action of
\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
preserves
S^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
is a graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
.

If
\imath_{v}\beta+\gamma(\Sigma(v))
preserves
S^{\prime}
for some (hence any) section
\Sigma
, Lemma
4.26
tells us that
i^{*}(\beta+\gamma)+\partial(\gamma\circ\Sigma)
is the image under
i_{*}
of a
(2,2)
-cocycle of
\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
; since
\beta
is
\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
-invariant, this cocycle is admissible. If it also integrable, we must show that
[S,S]=V\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
where the bracket is the deformed one defined by
i^{*}(\beta+\gamma)+\partial(\gamma\circ\Sigma)
. The odd-odd bracket is nothing but the map
\kappa+\gamma-\gamma\circ\Sigma\circ\kappa\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\to V\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
. Recall that the section gives
{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}=\mathfrak{D}\oplus\Sigma(V)}
as a vector space, so since we have

(\kappa+\gamma-\gamma\circ\Sigma\circ\kappa)(\mathfrak{D})=\gamma(\mathfrak{D})=\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}

by definition, and for
v\in V
,

(\kappa+\gamma-\gamma\circ\Sigma\circ\kappa)(\Sigma(v))=v+\gamma(\Sigma(v))-\gamma(\Sigma(v))=v,

we have
(\kappa+\gamma-\gamma\circ\Sigma\circ\kappa)\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}\bigr)=V\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
as required.

Now assume that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is an odd-generated realisable filtered subdeformation with associated graded
\mathfrak{a}=V\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
; by Lemma
4.26
, the associated admissible cohomology class has a representative
\alpha+\beta+\gamma
satisfying

i_{*}(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=i^{*}\bigl(\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)+\partial\bigl(\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma\bigr)

for some
\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
(which we take to be
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant by admissibility) and some section
\Sigma
. We will show that
\smash{\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma})}}
, and we note that this implies that
\bigl(S^{\prime},\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma}\bigr)
is a Lie pair and that
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma})}
. The odd-odd bracket
\kappa+\widehat{\gamma}-\widehat{\gamma}\circ\Sigma\circ\kappa
surjects onto
V\oplus\mathfrak{h}
. Therefore, if
A\in\mathfrak{h}
, there exists
\omega_{A}\in\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}
such that

A=\kappa(\omega_{A})+\widehat{\gamma}(\omega_{A})-\widehat{\gamma}(\Sigma(\kappa(\omega_{A})))\in V\oplus\mathfrak{h},

but since
A\in\mathfrak{h}
, we must have
\kappa(\omega_{A})=0
, whence
\omega_{A}\in\mathfrak{D}
. Conversely, for any
\omega\in\mathfrak{D}
we must have
\widehat{\gamma}(\omega)\in\mathfrak{h}
, whence
\mathfrak{h}=\widehat{\gamma}(\mathfrak{D})=\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\gamma})}
.
∎

With the result above in mind, we say that a Lie pair
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
is
admissible
if
\imath_{v}\beta+\gamma(\Sigma(v))
preserves
S^{\prime}
and
integrable
if it is admissible and the corresponding admissible cocycle is integrable.

4.5.4
Equivalence of Lie pairs and correspondence

Now fix
S^{\prime}
and a section
\Sigma\colon V\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}
, and suppose that
\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
; that is,
\beta|_{V\otimes S^{\prime}}=0
, which also implies that
\gamma|_{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S^{\prime}}=0
. Then
\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}=0
, and
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
is trivially an integrable Lie pair; we will call it a
trivial Lie pair
. For such a pair, the associated deformation obtained from Proposition
4.28
is trivial;
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}\cong\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
which has underlying vector space
{V\oplus S^{\prime}}
.

Note that if we have two admissible Lie pairs
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
and
(S^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})
such that
(\beta^{\prime}-\beta)+(\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma)\in\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(V\oplus S^{\prime})
(that is,
\beta^{\prime}|_{V\otimes S^{\prime}}=\beta|_{V\otimes S^{\prime}})
then
\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}=\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
, and
(\beta^{\prime}-\beta)+(\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma)
is invariant under the action of this algebra. Both Lie pairs define the same admissible cocycle in Proposition
4.28
; it follows that if one of these pairs is integrable, so is the other, and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
. Conversely, two Lie pairs which have the same envelope
\mathfrak{h}
and define the same admissible cocycle for the resulting graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
define an
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant element in
\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(V\oplus S^{\prime})
.

Now recall the natural action of
G=\GrAut\mathfrak{s}
on
\mathfrak{s}
. This action preserves the graded components of
\mathfrak{s}
as
\mathfrak{so}(V)
-modules and the Dirac current
\kappa
, whence it follows that
G
acts on the Spencer cochain spaces of
\mathfrak{s}
(via induced actions on duals and tensor products of
G
-modules) and that this action preserves the Spencer differential
\partial
, so
G
acts as automorphisms of the Spencer complex.

If
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
is a (resp. integrable, admissible) Lie pair and
g\in G
, then
(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))
is also a (resp. integrable, admissible) Lie pair with

\mathfrak{h}_{(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))}=g\cdot\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))}=g\cdot\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
.
Note that the map
g\cdot\Sigma:V\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}(g\cdot S^{\prime})
defined by
(g\cdot\Sigma)(v)=g\cdot\Sigma\bigl(g^{-1}\cdot v\bigr)
is a section of the Dirac current for
g\cdot S^{\prime}
. Using
\Sigma
and
g\cdot\Sigma
to construct the deformations of Proposition
4.28
, in the integrable case we can construct an isomorphism of subdeformations of
\mathfrak{s}

\Phi\colon\ \widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))}

as follows. Since we treat a filtered deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
as an alternative bracket structure on the same underlying space as its associated graded
\mathfrak{a}
, we have
\Phi=\Gr\Phi
as linear maps. Thus we may define
\Phi\colon\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}\to g\cdot\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
on the underlying graded vector spaces by
\Phi(X)=g\cdot X
for
X\in\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
. This preserves the grading, hence also the natural filtration (in the strong sense), and one can check that we have

\Phi(\commutator{X}{Y}_{\beta+\gamma+\partial(\gamma\circ\Sigma)})=\commutator{\Phi(X)}{\Phi(Y)}_{g\cdot\beta+g\cdot\gamma+\partial((g\cdot\gamma)\circ(g\cdot\Sigma))},

where the brackets are the deformed ones using the cocycle in the subscript. The inverse of
\Phi
is of course given by the action of
g^{-1}
, which also preserves all structure, hence
\Phi
is indeed an isomorphism of subdeformations.

The observations above suggest putting an equivalence relation on Lie pairs; in fact, we will define two different such notions.

Definition 4.29
.

Two Lie pairs
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
and
(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})
are
weakly equivalent
if
(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})=(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma)+\phi)
for some
g\in G
and
\phi\in\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(V\oplus S^{\prime\prime})^{\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}}
, and they are
strongly equivalent
if they are weakly equivalent and
\phi=0
.

These notions of equivalence are a generalisation of the equivalence relation on Lie pairs for the 11-dimensional Poincaré superalgebra found in
[
32
,
56
]
. As previously noted, in those works
{G=\Spin(V)}
rather than
\GrAut
and the
\phi
term does not appear in the equivalence relation since
\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(V\oplus S^{\prime})=0
for all
S^{\prime}
, so there is no distinction between strong and weak equivalence. We will see that weak equivalence is a more natural concept than strong equivalence, but strong equivalence will give us some additional information.

Fixing a Lie pair
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
, we define

\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}:=\{A\in\mathfrak{so}(V)\mid A\cdot S^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}\text{ and }A\cdot\beta=0\}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(V);

note that by definition of a Lie pair, this algebra contains the envelope
\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
, and

\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}=V\oplus S^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}

is a graded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{s}
containing
\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
; in fact, it is the maximal such subalgebra whose odd part is
S^{\prime}
and whose zero-graded part preserves
\beta
.

If
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
is integrable, then the integrable cohomology class of
\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
corresponding to the Lie pair pushes forward to an integrable cohomology class of
\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
which defines a geometrically realisable filtered deformation
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
which we call
maximal
(with respect to
{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
), since it is maximal among realisable subdeformations into which
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
embeds as the odd-generated ideal.

Proposition 4.30
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between weak equivalence classes of integrable Lie pairs
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
and isomorphism classes of highly supersymmetric odd-generated realisable subdeformations of
\mathfrak{s}
.

If
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma),(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})
are
strongly
equivalent Lie pairs, then their maximal realisable subdeformations
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})},\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
are isomorphic as subdeformations.

Proof.

The discussion above Definition
4.29
shows that weakly equivalent integrable Lie pairs define isomorphic odd-generated realisable subdeformations. By the converse part of Proposition
4.28
, every odd-generated realisable subdeformation is induced by a Lie pair. It remains to show that if the Lie pairs
(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)
and
(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})
are such that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}\cong\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}
as subdeformations then they are weakly equivalent. If
\Phi\colon\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}
is such an isomorphism,
\Gr\Phi\colon\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}\to\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}
is given by
x\mapsto g\cdot x
for
g\in G
, so

\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}=g\cdot\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}=\mathfrak{a}_{(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))}.

But we have already seen that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}\cong\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))}
for all
g\in G
, with the associated graded isomorphism of graded superalgebras given by the action of
g
, so we must also have
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}\cong\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))}
with the associated graded isomorphism being the identity map on
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}:=g\cdot\mathfrak{a}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
. It follows from Proposition
4.13
that the admissible cohomology classes in
\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{-},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})
associated to the two deformations are equal, whence their images under
i_{*}\colon\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{-},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{-},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
are also equal, i.e., we have

i^{*}[\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}]=i^{*}[g\cdot\beta+g\cdot\gamma]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{-},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}).

Thus we find that
\phi:=(\beta^{\prime}-g\cdot\beta)+(\gamma^{\prime}-g\cdot\gamma)\in\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{-}=V\oplus S^{\prime\prime})
, and
\phi
is invariant under the action of the envelope
\mathfrak{h}_{(S^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})}
since
\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}
and
g\cdot\beta+g\cdot\gamma
both are.

For the strong case, we note that
g\cdot\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}=\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(g\cdot S^{\prime},g\cdot(\beta+\gamma))}
, whence it follows that strongly equivalent Lie pairs define isomorphic maximal subdeformations by an entirely analogous argument to the weak case.
∎

We note that there does not seem to be a converse statement to the second part of the result above; for this to hold, we would have to show that
\phi
as in the proof vanishes if it is
g\cdot\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{max}}_{(S^{\prime},\beta+\gamma)}
-invariant, which there does not seem to be a reason to expect. Nonetheless, we have reduced the problem of classifying odd-generated realisable subdeformations of
\mathfrak{s}
up to isomorphism to classifying integrable Lie pairs up to weak equivalence, and we can also obtain all maximal realisables with respect to a weak equivalence class from the strong equivalence classes it contains.

4.5.5
Maximally supersymmetric case

The case
S^{\prime}=S
brings a number of simplifications to the above discussion. First, for any
\beta+\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{2,2}
,
(S,\beta+\gamma)
is a Lie pair if and only if the envelope
\mathfrak{h}_{(S,\beta+\gamma)}
preserves
\beta
, and any Lie pair is admissible (recall our previous observation that if
\mathfrak{a}
is maximally supersymmetric, all
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
-invariant
(2,2)
-cohomology classes are admissible), although one still must check that it is integrable. Since
\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(V\otimes S)=0
, there is no distinction between strong and weak equivalence of Lie pairs; to any equivalence class of integrable Lie pairs there corresponds a unique (up to isomorphism) odd-generated realisable subdeformation and a unique maximal realisable subdeformation containing it.

5
Highly supersymmetric Lorentzian spin manifolds

In this section, we invert the perspective of Section
3
: rather than constructing a filtered subdeformation of a flat model
\mathfrak{s}
from sections on a spin manifold (namely the Killing superalgebra), we seek to begin with such a filtered subdeformation and construct a spin manifold with admissible connection on which the elements of the algebra can be realised as sections, in doing so providing a partial converse to Theorem
3.10
. We restrict ourselves to the highly supersymmetric Lorentzian case, in which the homogeneity theorem (see Theorem
4.8
) provides significant simplifications as it did in Section
4
(although see Remark
4.11
), and our results will ultimately justify the approach of that section by demonstrating that our notion of geometric realisability (see Definition
4.17
) is a good one. We will essentially generalise the results of
[
32
, Section 3.3]
. We make extensive use of the conventions and results related to homogeneous spaces discussed in Appendix
A.4
. We begin by establishing how we will deal with spinors on homogeneous manifolds.

5.1
Homogeneous spin structures

Spin structures on homogeneous manifolds were first treated systematically by Bär
[
5
]
and by Cahen et al.
[
13
]
; our present treatment follows
[
32
]
.

Let
(G,H,\eta)
be a metric Klein pair (of any signature), let
(M=G/H,g)
be the associated homogeneous space, which we assume to be oriented, and let
V=T_{o}M
where
o=H
. Suppose that there exists a lift of the isotropy representation
H\to\SO(V)
to
\Spin(V)
; that is, a Lie group morphism
H\to\Spin(V)
making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spin(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SO(V).}

Then the canonical map
\Spin(V)\to\SO(V)
induces a
\Spin(V)
-equivariant bundle map

G\times_{H}\Spin(V)\to G\times_{H}\SO(V)\cong F_{\rm SO},

which is a spin structure on
M
, where we recall that
F_{\rm SO}
is the special orthonormal frame bundle. In order to define the isomorphism we have implicitly chosen an orthonormal frame over
o
(as discussed in Appendix
A.4.2
). We call this the
homogeneous spin structure associated to the lift
H\to\Spin(V)
. Note that
G
(left) acts naturally on both of these bundles compatibly with the right action by the spin group, and that the bundle isomorphism is
G
-equivariant.

Using the isotropy representation and related representations of
H
, many natural bundles on
M
, particularly those associated to
F_{\rm SO}
via representations of
\SO(V)
, can be viewed as associated bundles to the
H
-principal bundle
G\to M
. Homogeneous spin structures allow us to do the same with representations of the spin group; for example, if
S
is a spinor module of
\Spin(V)
, there is a
G
-equivariant isomorphism of vector bundles

\underline{S}:=(G\times_{H}\Spin(V))\times_{\Spin(V)}S\cong G\times_{H}S.

If
G
is simply connected, the construction above classifies the spin structures on
(M,g)
in definite signature.

Lemma 5.1

(
[
5
,
13
]
)
.

If
(M,g)
is a homogeneous Riemannian
G
-space where
G
is simply connected, the equivalence classes of spin structures on
M
are in one-to-one correspondence with spin lifts of the isotropy representation.

The proof of this result relies on the ability to lift the action of
G
on
F_{\rm SO}
induced by the action on
M
to an action on an arbitrary spin structure
P\to M
which can then be localised at
o
to find a lift of the isotropy representation. In definite signature, this follows from the fact that the spin group is connected and simply connected (for
\dim V\geq 3
), but it fails in indefinite signature due to the non-trivial topology of the spin group; in signature
(p\geq 1,q\geq 1)
, the spin group is disconnected, and its connected component is not simply connected for
(p\geq 2,q\geq 2)
. However, in the case that
M
is a
reductive

G
-space, the above still holds in arbitrary signature
[
1
,
13
]
.

To avoid these issues, one can simply hypothesise that the action of
G
lifts to the spin structure; we then say that we have a
homogeneous spin structure
. Such structures are in one-to-one correspondence with lifts of the isotropy representation.

5.2
Reconstruction of highly supersymmetric backgrounds

Let us now turn directly to the question of constructing a background geometry on which a filtered deformation of a Poincaré superalgebra can be realised. Let
(V,\eta)
be a Lorentzian inner product space,
S
a (possibly
N
-extended) spinor module of
\Spin(V)
,
\kappa\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}S\to V
a symmetric and causal Dirac current and
\mathfrak{s}
the Poincaré superalgebra defined by this data (see Definition
2.1
). Our ultimate goal is to generalise
[
32
, Theorem 13]
, which says that geometrically realisable highly supersymmetric filtered subdeformations of the 11-dimensional Poincaré superalgebra are subalgebras of Killing superalgebras. We will find some potential obstructions to doing this in full generality, but the obstructions themselves will prove instructive.

5.2.1
Super Harish-Chandra pairs

We begin by introducing what can be viewed as a Lie supergroup associated to a Lie superalgebra. In a superspace formalism, the supersymmetric backgrounds we will construct could be considered as homogeneous spaces for such a supergroup. We will not fully use such a formalism, but the following definition will be useful for us. See
[
55
]
and the references therein for more on the superspace perspective.

Definition 5.2

(super Harish-Chandra pair)
.

A
super Harish-Chandra pair
is a pair
(G_{\overline{0}},\mathfrak{g})
where
\mathfrak{g}
is a finite-dimensional Lie superalgebra and
G_{\overline{0}}
is a connected Lie group integrating
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
equipped with a representation
\Ad\colon G_{\overline{0}}\to\GL(\mathfrak{g})
(which we call the
adjoint representation
of the pair) integrating the restriction
\ad\colon\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
of the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}
to
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
.

Note that if
(G_{\overline{0}},\mathfrak{g})
is a super Harish-Chandra pair, the adjoint representation
\Ad\colon G_{\overline{0}}\to\GL(\mathfrak{g})
preserves the grading on
\mathfrak{g}
since
\ad
does. The induced subrepresentation on
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
is simply the adjoint representation of
G_{\overline{0}}
.

Let us now consider two motivating examples.

Example 5.3
.

The spin cover
\Spin_{0}(V)\ltimes V
of the connected Poincaré group
\SO_{0}(V)\ltimes V
of course has the Poincaré algebra
\mathfrak{s}_{\overline{0}}=\mathfrak{iso}(V)
as its Lie algebra, and equipping it with the action
(a,v)\cdot s=a\cdot s
on
\mathfrak{s}_{\overline{1}}=S
makes
(\Spin_{0}(V)\ltimes V,\mathfrak{s})
into a super Harish-Chandra pair.

Example 5.4
.

Let
(M,g,D)
be a connected Lorentzian spin manifold with admissible connection and let
\mathfrak{K}_{D}
be its Killing superalgebra (see Definition
3.6
). Let us further assume that this is
highly supersymmetric
, in the sense that
\dim\mathfrak{S}_{D}>\frac{1}{2}\dim S
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{K}_{D}
is highly supersymmetric as a filtered subdeformation of
\mathfrak{s}
). Then, by Theorem
4.8
, the values of the Killing vector fields arising as squares of Killing spinors span the tangent space at every point of
M
. Thus
M
is
locally homogeneous
; informally, it locally looks like a homogeneous space for a group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{V}_{D}
. If we further assume that
M
is geodesically complete,
\mathfrak{V}_{D}
integrates to a connected subgroup
G
of
\ISO(M,g)
which acts transitively on
M

[
62
, Theorem 2.2]
, whence
M
is in fact homogeneous. By pulling back the action of
G
to its universal covering group
\widetilde{G}
, it follows that
(M,g)
is a homogeneous Lorentzian spin manifold for
\widetilde{G}
. Since
\widetilde{G}
is simply connected, the restriction
\ad\colon\mathfrak{V}_{D}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{K}_{D})
of the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{K}_{D}
to
\mathfrak{V}_{D}
integrates to a representation
\Ad\colon\widetilde{G}\to\mathfrak{K}_{D}
. Thus
(\widetilde{G},\mathfrak{K}_{D})
is a super Harish-Chandra pair.

The second example in particular shows us a way forward: where a Killing superalgebra is highly supersymmetric, the background which supports it is locally homogeneous, and if it is actually homogeneous, one can associate to it a super Harish-Chandra pair containing the Killing superalgebra.

Consider now a highly supersymmetric filtered subdeformation
\mathfrak{g}
of the Poincaré superalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
, say
\Gr\mathfrak{g}\cong\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{s}
as graded Lie superalgebras so that
V=\mathfrak{a}_{-2}
,
S^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{a}_{-1}
and
\mathfrak{h}:=\mathfrak{a}_{0}
as usual. In particular, the filtration on
\mathfrak{g}
is of the form
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}^{-2}\supseteq\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\supseteq\mathfrak{g}^{0}\supseteq\mathfrak{g}^{1}=0
and as Lie algebras,
\mathfrak{h}=\Gr_{0}\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}^{0}/\mathfrak{g}^{1}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{0}
, so there is a canonical embedding of
\mathfrak{h}
as the zeroth part of the filtration of
\mathfrak{g}
.
19
19

19

This can also be seen by considering an explicit presentation of
\mathfrak{g}
as a filtered deformation of
\mathfrak{a}
.

Note also that the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{a}^{0}
preserves each
\mathfrak{g}^{k}
so that
\mathfrak{g}
is also filtered as an
\mathfrak{g}^{0}
-module, whence
\Gr\mathfrak{g}
is also naturally a graded
\mathfrak{g}^{0}
-module, and this action is the same one induced by the isomorphism
\mathfrak{h}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{0}
. We will thus identify
\mathfrak{g}^{0}
and
\mathfrak{h}
, in particular treating
\mathfrak{h}
as a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
(G_{\overline{0}},\mathfrak{g})
be a super Harish-Chandra pair where
\mathfrak{g}
is a highly supersymmetric filtered subdeformation of
\mathfrak{s}
, and suppose that the connected subgroup
H
of
G_{\overline{0}}
generated by
{\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{g}^{0}}
is closed. Then the inclusion
\mathfrak{h}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{so}(V)
integrates to a morphism
H\to\Spin(V)
. In particular, this lifts the isotropy representation and thus defines a homogeneous spin structure for
(G_{\overline{0}},H,\eta)
.

Proof.

First note that
V\cong\mathfrak{g}^{-2}/\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}/\mathfrak{h}
and
S^{\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{-1}/\mathfrak{g}^{0}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}}
as
\mathfrak{h}
-modules. In particular, since
\eta
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant,
(\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}},\mathfrak{h},\eta)
is a metric Lie pair, so
(G_{\overline{0}},H,\eta)
is a metric Klein pair, and the inclusion
\mathfrak{h}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{so}(V)
is the isotropy representation of
\mathfrak{h}
. Similarly, at the level of groups, the adjoint representation induces a Lie group morphism
\varphi\colon H\to\SO(V)
integrating
\mathfrak{h}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{so}(V)
. This lifts to a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{H}\to\Spin(V)
where
\pi_{H}\colon\widetilde{H}\to H
is the universal cover of
H
. We will show that there exists a diagonal arrow making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spin(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SO(V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commute, which proves the result. Recalling that there is a canonical short exact sequence of Lie groups

1\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(H)\longrightarrow\widetilde{H}\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow 1
,
we will show that
\pi_{1}(H)\subseteq\ker\tilde{\varphi}
, hence
\tilde{\varphi}
factors through
H
, giving the required arrow in the diagram.

Note that the actions of
H
on
V
and
S^{\prime}
pull back to actions of
\widetilde{H}
(integrating the actions of
\mathfrak{h}
) in which
\pi_{1}(H)
acts trivially. On the other hand,
\widetilde{H}
also acts on
V
and
S
via the action of
\Spin(V)
on those spaces. The two actions of
\widetilde{H}
on
V
agree by the commutation of the square diagram above. Since
\tilde{\varphi}
integrates
\mathfrak{h}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{so}(V)
and the action of
\mathfrak{h}
(via
\mathfrak{so}(V)
) on
S
preserves
S^{\prime}
, so too does the action of
\widetilde{H}
via
\Spin(V)
. The two actions of
\widetilde{H}
on
S^{\prime}
both integrate the same action of
\mathfrak{h}
on
S^{\prime}
, so they agree.

The trivial action of
\pi_{1}(H)
on
V
gives us

\pi_{1}(H)\subseteq\ker(\varphi\circ\pi_{H})=\ker(\pi\circ\tilde{\varphi})

thus
\tilde{\varphi}(\pi_{1}(H))\subseteq\ker\pi=\{\pm\mathbbm{1}\}
. On the other hand, the trivial action of
\pi_{1}(H)
on
S^{\prime}
shows that
-\mathbbm{1}\notin\tilde{\varphi}(\pi_{1}(H))
, thus
\pi_{1}(H)\subseteq\ker\tilde{\varphi}
. This shows that
\tilde{\varphi}
factors through
H
as claimed.
∎

5.2.2
Reconstruction theorem

We can now state and prove our final result which is a partial converse to Theorem
3.10
and generalisation of
[
32
, Theorem 13]
, with some caveats. Since this result essentially allows one to (locally) reconstruct a highly supersymmetric background from its Killing superalgebra, we follow
[
32
,
56
]
and refer to it as the
Reconstruction Theorem
.

Theorem 5.6

(reconstruction of highly supersymmetric background)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a geometrically realisable highly supersymmetric filtered subdeformation of the Poincaré superalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
.
Let
G_{\overline{0}}
be the connected and simply connected Lie group corresponding to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
and suppose that the connected subgroup
H
corresponding to
\mathfrak{h}
is closed.
Then there exists a homogeneous spin structure on the Lorentzian homogeneous space
M=G_{\overline{0}}/H
with a Dirac current
\kappa
and a connection
D
on the spinor bundle
\underline{S}=G_{\overline{0}}\times_{H}S
as well as an injective
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded linear map
{\Psi\colon\mathfrak{g}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{V}_{D}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}}
which restricts to a Lie algebra embedding
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{V}_{D}
and which satisfies

\displaystyle\Psi(\commutator{X}{s})=\mathscr{L}_{\Psi(X)}\Psi(s),\qquad\Psi\quantity(\comm{s}{s'})=\kappa\quantity(\Psi(s),\Psi(s'))

(5.1)

for all
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
and
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}}
.

Furthermore, if
\Psi(\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}})=\mathfrak{S}_{D}
or if
D
is admissible in the sense of Definition
3.6
otherwise, we find in particular that
\mathfrak{g}
embeds into the Killing superalgebra
\mathfrak{K}_{D}
of
(M,g,D)
.

Remark 5.7
.

Let us make some comments about this result before giving the proof. First, it is necessary to hypothesise that
H
is closed; it may be possible to adjust the choice of
\mathfrak{h}
such that this is the case but we have not been able to show this. We note that this is also implicitly assumed in
[
32
, Theorem 13]
; although it is not in the statement of the theorem, it is stated in the preamble to the theorem.

Moreover, the present theorem is very close to saying that
all
geometrically realisable highly supersymmetric subdeformations of
\mathfrak{s}
embed into Killing superalgebras, but there is an obstruction in that it does not seem to be guaranteed that the connection
D
on
\underline{S}
which we will construct in the proof is admissible. The algebraic conditions (1) and (2) of Definition
3.6
are satisfied because we work with admissible cocycles which are expressed in terms of normalised cocycles of the whole Poincaré superalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
; the difficulty is in showing condition (3), namely that
\mathscr{L}_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}\beta=0
for all
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}
; we can only show this for
\epsilon\in\Psi(\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}})
. On the other hand, by a dimension count we find that
maximally
supersymmetric subdeformations do in fact embed in Killing superalgebras, since in this case we must have
\Psi(\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}})=\mathfrak{S}_{D}
.

To put a positive spin on this issue, it provides us with new motivation to study Killing superalgebras constructed from
constrained
Killing spinors, which we briefly discussed in Section
3.3
; while
D
might not be guaranteed to satisfy the conditions for admissibility on sections of
\underline{S}
, it
does
satisfy these conditions on sections of the sub-bundle
\underline{S}^{\prime}:=G_{\overline{0}}\times_{H}S^{\prime}
to which elements of
\Psi(\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}})
belong and which we could describe as the kernel of some fibrewise linear operator on
\underline{S}
.

For the proof, we will make use of the technology for working with connections on homogeneous spaces presented in Appendix
A.4.4
, namely Nomizu maps and Wang’s theorem (see Theorem
A.6
). If we have a spin-lift
\tilde{\varphi}
of the isotropy representation, then
{P=G_{\overline{0}}\times_{H}\Spin(V)}
is a
G_{\overline{0}}
-invariant principal
\Spin(V)
-bundle and
\tilde{\varphi}
is a map from the isotropy group to the structure group of
P
– to connect with the notation in Theorem
A.6
, we have
G=G_{\overline{0}}
,
K=\Spin(V)
and
\psi=\tilde{\varphi}
. Then one can easily check that the Nomizu map
{L\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{so}(V)}
of the Levi-Civita connection (considered as a
G_{\overline{0}}
-invariant principal connection on
F_{\rm SO}
) satisfies the conditions of Wang’s theorem so also corresponds with a principal connection on
P
, which is of course the spin-lift of the Levi-Civita connection.

Proof.

By hypothesis, we have a metric Klein pair
(G_{\overline{0}},H,\eta)
. Then by Lemma
5.5
, there exists a spin lift of the isotropy representation of
(G_{\overline{0}},H,\eta)
with corresponding homogeneous spin structure
P=G_{\overline{0}}\times_{H}\Spin(V)
and spinor bundle
\underline{S}=G_{\overline{0}}\times_{H}S
on
M=G_{\overline{0}}/H
. We identify
V
with
T_{o}M\cong\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
. Since
H
is connected, the image of the spin-lift of the isotropy representation lies in
\Spin_{0}(V)
, whence
(M,g)
is time-orientable and so we can construct a bundle Dirac current
{\kappa\colon\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{2}\underline{S}\to TM}
by Lemma
3.3
.

Now let
[\mu_{-}]
be the admissible Spencer cohomology class for
\mathfrak{a}=\Gr\mathfrak{g}\subseteq\mathfrak{s}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{g}
, so that there exists
\beta+\gamma\in\bigl(\mathcal{H}^{2,2}\bigr)^{\mathfrak{h}}
satisfying
i_{*}[\mu_{-}]=i^{*}[\beta+\gamma]\in\mathsf{H}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-};\mathfrak{s})
. Recall that
{\beta\in\Hom(V\otimes S,S)\cong V^{*}\otimes\End S}
is
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant and thus
H
-invariant, so by Frobenius reciprocity (see Theorem
A.5
) it defines a unique
G_{\overline{0}}
-invariant 1-form with values in
\End\underline{S}
, although we will actually adopt a sign convention where we work with
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Omega^{1}(M;\End\underline{S})^{G_{\overline{0}}}
corresponding to
-\beta
. We define the connection
D:=\nabla-\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

Since
G_{\overline{0}}
acts by isometries by construction and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is
G_{\overline{0}}
-equivariant, for each
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
the fundamental vector field
\xi^{M}_{X}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is Killing and preserves
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, so it lies in
\mathfrak{V}_{D}
. Recalling that the assignment
X\to\xi^{M}_{X}
is a Lie algebra
anti
-homomorphism
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
, we define
\Psi(X)=-\xi^{M}_{X}
. This gives us a Lie algebra morphism
\Psi\colon\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}\to\mathfrak{V}_{D}
.

To construct the spinor fields associated to elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}}=S^{\prime}
and show that they are
D
-parallel, we will exploit the homogeneous bundle structure of
S
. Let us define a left action of
H
on
G_{\overline{0}}
by
h\cdot g=gh^{-1}
where we use group multiplication in
G_{\overline{0}}
on the right-hand side. Then, using some standard associated bundle theory, we have identifications between spaces of sections and
H
-equivariant maps

\mathfrak{X}(M)\cong C^{\infty}\quantity(G_{\overline 0};V)^{H},\qquad\mathfrak{S}\cong C^{\infty}\quantity(G_{\overline 0};S)^{H}.

For
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
, the fundamental vector field
\xi^{M}_{X}
corresponds to the map
G_{\overline{0}}\to V\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}/\mathfrak{h}
given by
X\mapsto\overline{\Ad_{g^{-1}}X}
(where the overline denotes the image under the quotient by
\mathfrak{h}
). Similarly, for
s\in\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}}
we define
\Psi(s)\in\mathfrak{S}
as the spinor field associated to the map
G_{\overline{0}}\to S
defined by
{g\mapsto\Ad_{g^{-1}}s}
. We now use Wang’s theorem and the Levi-Civita Nomizu map to show that these spinor fields are
D
-parallel. First, let us take a presentation of the form (
4.24
) for
\mathfrak{g}
; in particular, we have a (non-reductive) split
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus V
and a map
\lambda\colon V\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
. Then, writing elements
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
in the form
X=A+v
for
A\in\mathfrak{h}
,
v\in V
, we can follow the construction of the Nomizu map
L\colon\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
corresponding to the Levi-Civita connection (see Appendix
A.4.4
) to compute

L(A+v)=A+\lambda(v)
.
In particular, for
s\in S^{\prime}
we have
L(A+v)\cdot s=\commutator{A+v}{s}-\beta(v,s)
, where we use the definition of the bracket on
\mathfrak{g}
. Since this is also the map corresponding to the spin-lift of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
under Wang’s theorem, we can compute the
H
-equivariant map
G_{\overline{0}}\to S
corresponding to the spinor field
\nabla_{\xi^{M}_{X}}\Psi(s)
as

g\mapsto(L(\Ad_{g^{-1}}X)-\ad_{\Ad_{g^{-1}}X})\quantity(\Ad_{g^{-1}}s)=-\beta\quantity(\overline{\Ad_{g^{-1}}X},\Ad_{g^{-1}}s),

where the equality is due to the expression for
L
above. But this is nothing but the map corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigl(\xi^{M}_{X},\Psi(s)\bigr)
(recalling our sign convention for
\boldsymbol{\beta}
). By homogeneity, the values of the fundamental vector fields span every tangent space, so we have shown that
\nabla\Psi(s)=\boldsymbol{\beta}\Psi(s)
, whence
\Psi(s)\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}
as required. We now have a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded map
\Psi\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{V}_{D}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{D}
which can show satisfies the equations (
5.1
) by examining the Killing transport data at the basepoint (see Section
3.2
, in particular the explicit presentation of the brackets of the Killing superalgebra as a filtered deformation (
3.12
)). This will also show that
\Psi
is injective since no non-zero element of
\mathfrak{g}
has trivial transport data.

Finally, we note that if
D
is admissible,
\Psi\colon\mathfrak{g}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{K}_{D}
is a Lie superalgebra embedding. The Spencer cocycle conditions for
\beta+\gamma
ensure
\boldsymbol{\beta}
satisfies conditions (1) and (2) of Definition
3.6
. Condition (3) (
\mathscr{L}_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}\boldsymbol{\beta}=0
for all
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}
) is not automatically satisfied, but observe that we have
\mathscr{L}_{\xi^{M}_{X}}\boldsymbol{\beta}=0
for all
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
by
G_{\overline{0}}
-invariance of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
; in particular,
\mathscr{L}_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}\boldsymbol{\beta}=0
holds for
{\epsilon\in\Psi(\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}})}
.
∎

We note that we have glossed over some details of the calculations above, in particular when constructing the spinorial part of the map
\Psi
; these details are routine exercises in principal connections but somewhat cumbersome to write out in full. A more elegant way of dealing with them is through the superspace formalism introduced in
[
55
]
which is used, somewhat implicitly, in the proof of
[
32
, Theorem 13]
, but which is beyond the scope of this work.

Remark 5.8
.

We have already noted some differences between the result above and the much cleaner statement in the 11-dimensional case. Another difference to be aware of is that a geometry
(M,g,D)
does not seem to be determined by the odd-generated ideal
\commutator{\fg_{\overline 1}}{\fg_{\overline 1}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}}
(or weak equivalence class of a maximal Lie pair
(S^{\prime}=\underline{S}_{p},\beta)
) in general since we may have
\mathcal{K}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{a}_{-})\neq 0
; the discussion of Section
4.5
shows that if this is the case then it is possible for two realisable subdeformations of
\mathfrak{s}
to have the same odd-generated ideal but for neither to embed in the other. On the geometric side, this means that two simply connected backgrounds may be isometric to one another but have different admissible connections
D
(corresponding to different flux configurations, in supergravity terminology). On the other hand, it is not hard to see that a
strong
equivalence class of a maximal Lie pairs
does
determine the connection uniquely.

Appendix A
Further background

A.1
Inner product spaces and spinors

For the sake of brevity, we will not give a detailed account of the theory of Clifford algebras, spin groups and spinors here; standard references include
[
41
,
47
]
; see also
[
25
]
. We will briefly establish some conventions, however. Throughout, let
(V,\eta)
be a (pseudo-)inner product space.
We recall that using
\eta
, the vector spaces
V
and
V^{*}
can be identified via the
musical isomorphisms

{\flat\colon V\to V^{*}}
,
v\mapsto v^{\flat}
where

v^{\flat}(w)=\eta(v,w)

for all
v,w\in V
and
\sharp\colon V^{*}\to V
,
\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\sharp}
which is defined as the inverse of
\flat
.

A.1.1
Orthogonal groups and algebras

We define the
orthogonal group
of
(V,\eta)
as the group of linear automorphisms of
V
which preserve
\eta
,

\Orth(V,\eta):=\{A\in\GL(V)\mid\forall v,w\in V,\,\eta(Av,Aw)=\eta(v,w)\},

and the
special orthogonal group
as the subgroup of elements of
\Orth(V,\eta)
which preserve orientations,

\SO(V,\eta):=\{A\in\Orth(V)\mid\det A=1\}
,
though we typically suppress the inner product
\eta
in the notation where there is no ambiguity. The latter group is a normal subgroup of the former of index 2. Some basic topological properties depend on the signature of
\eta
as follows.

•

If
\eta
is (positive- or negative-)definite,
\Orth(V)
has two connected components, with the connected component of the identity being
\SO(V)
, and both groups are compact.

•

If
\eta
is not definite,
\Orth(V)
has four connected components, two of which constitute
\SO(V)
. We denote the connected component of the identity by
\SO_{0}(V)
.
20
20

20

Following naming conventions used for the Lorentzian case in physics, this group is sometimes called the
proper orthochronous orthogonal group
. It consists of invertible linear isometries of
(V,\eta)
which preserve both orientations and time orientations on
V
.
None of these groups are compact.

Clearly, all of these groups share the same Lie algebra, which can be identified with the Lie algebra of
\eta
-skew-symmetric endomorphisms

\mathfrak{so}(V):=\{A\in\End(V)\mid\eta(Av,w)+\eta(v,Aw)=0\}

under the commutator bracket. We also define
\Orth(p,q):=\Orth(\mathbb{R}^{p,q})
etc., where
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
is the standard inner product space with signature
(p,q)
; that is, with
p
positive eigenvalues and
q
negative eigenvalues.

A.1.2
Clifford algebras

The defining relation for the Clifford algebra
\Cl(V,\eta)
is

v\cdot v=\pm\eta(v,v)\mathbbm{1}

(A.1)

for all
v\in V
. Note that there is a choice of sign convention here; the
+
is more common in the physics literature while the
-
is usually preferred in mathematics. We adopt the mathematical convention with the
-
sign in defining
\Cl(p,q):=\Cl(\mathbb{R}^{p,q})
. Any inner product space is (non-canonically) isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
for some unique pair
(p,q)
, and such an isomorphism induces an isomorphism of associative algebras
\Cl(V,\eta)\cong\Cl(p,q)
. In previous works on supersymmetry and Spencer cohomology
[
10
,
22
,
23
,
33
]
and in the upcoming work treating 2-dimensional examples mentioned in the introduction, a
+
sign is adopted for calculational convenience. We also typically suppress
\eta
in the notation for the Clifford algebra (and the related groups defined below), writing
\Cl(V)=\Cl(V,\eta)
.

There is an isomorphism of vector spaces
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}V\simeq\Cl(V,\eta)
which is most easily described in terms of an orthonormal basis
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{\mu}\}_{\mu=1}^{\dim V}
, although it is actually independent of this choice; to each exterior product of the form
\boldsymbol{e}_{\mu_{1}}\wedge\boldsymbol{e}_{\mu_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\boldsymbol{e}_{\mu_{r}}
, we associate the Clifford product
{\boldsymbol{e}_{\mu_{1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{\mu_{2}}\cdot\cdots\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{\mu_{r}}}
. We thus identify these two spaces and view Clifford multiplication
\cdot
as a new algebra structure on the exterior algebra
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}V
.

The exterior algebra
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}V
has a natural
\mathbb{Z}
-grading by form degree. Clifford multiplication does not respect this grading, but it does respect the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading given by taking the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading modulo 2. We denote the even subalgebra of the Clifford algebra
(
which is generated by the subspace corresponding to
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V
)
by
\Cl_{\overline{0}}
.

There is a vector space embedding
\omega\colon\mathfrak{so}(V)\hookrightarrow\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}V
whose image is
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}V
given by
A\mapsto\pm\frac{1}{2}\omega_{A}
where
\omega_{A}
is the 2-vector defined by the equation

\imath_{v^{\flat}}\omega_{A}=-Av

where
\imath_{v^{\flat}}
is contraction with the musical image
v^{\flat}\in V^{*}
of an arbitrary vector
v\in V
.
This an embedding of Lie algebras if
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}V
is equipped with the commutator Lie bracket with respect to Clifford multiplication.

A.1.3
Pinor and spinor representations

The Clifford algebra
\Cl(V)
has either a single isomorphism class of irreducible real finite-dimensional modules or two such classes, depending on
p-q\mod 4
where
(p,q)
is the signature of
(V,\eta)
.

•

For
p-q\neq 3\mod 4
, there is a unique module which we denote by
\mathsf{S}
.

•

For
p-q=3\mod 4
, there are two such modules which can be distinguished by the fact that a unit volume element in
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{p+q}V
acts as
+\mathbbm{1}
(the identity) on one of these modules and
-\mathbbm{1}
on the other; given an orientation on
V
, we denote by
\mathsf{S}
the module on which the canonical unit volume element acts as
+\mathbbm{1}
.

All of these are referred to as (irreducible, real)
pinor
modules. Under the induced action of the even subalgebra
\Cl_{\overline{0}}(V)
, or equivalently the spin group (see below), the pinor modules may be reducible, depending on
p-q\mod 8
.

•

For
p-q=3,5,7\mod 8
,
\mathsf{S}
is irreducible; for later convenience we define
\mathsf{S}_{1}:=\mathsf{S}
in this case.

•

For
p-q=1,2,6
,
\mathsf{S}
has a decomposition into irreducible
\Cl_{\overline{0}}(V)
-modules
\mathsf{S}=\mathsf{S}_{+}\oplus\mathsf{S}_{-}
which happen to be isomorphic (as real representations); let us set
\mathsf{S}_{1}:=\mathsf{S}_{+}\cong\mathsf{S}_{-}
here.

•

For
p-q=0,4\mod 8
, there is a similar decomposition
\mathsf{S}=\mathsf{S}_{+}\oplus\mathsf{S}_{-}
, except here
\mathsf{S}_{+}\not\cong\mathsf{S}_{-}
. In any case,
\mathsf{S}_{1}
or
\mathsf{S}_{\pm}
are called the (irreducible, real)
spinor
modules.

A.1.4
Pin and spin groups

We define the
pin group
and
spin group
as the subgroups

\displaystyle\Pin(V)=\{v_{1}\cdot v_{2}\cdot\dots\cdot v_{r}\mid v_{i}\in V,\,\eta(v_{i},v_{i})=\pm 1\},

\displaystyle\Spin(V)=\{v_{1}\cdot v_{2}\cdot\dots\cdot v_{2r}\mid v_{i}\in V,\,\eta(v_{i},v_{i})=\pm 1\}=\Pin(V)\cap\Cl_{\overline{0}},

of the group of units
\Cl(V)^{\times}
. The spin group is a normal subgroup of the pin group of index 2, and similarly to the orthogonal groups, we have the following:

•

If
\eta
is definite,
\Pin(V)
has two connected components, with the connected component of the identity being
\Spin(V)
, and both groups are compact.

•

If
\eta
is not definite,
\Pin(V)
has four connected components, two of which constitute
\Spin(V)
. We denote the connected component of the identity by
\Spin_{0}(V)
. None of these groups are compact.

There is a canonical double-covering of Lie groups
\Pin(V)\to\Orth(V)
which restricts to double-coverings
\Spin(V)\to\SO(V)
and (where the groups exist)
\Spin_{0}(V)\to\SO_{0}(V)
. These covering maps differentiate to a canonical isomorphism of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{spin}_{\overline{0}}(V)=\mathfrak{spin}(V)\to\mathfrak{so}(V)
, thus we will identify these algebras.

The pin and spin groups act on the pinor modules by restriction of the action of the Clifford algebra. The irreducible pinor module
\mathsf{S}
is irreducible under the action of
\Pin(V)
. It is irreducible under the action of
\Spin(V)
or
\Spin_{0}(V)
if and only if it is irreducible under
\Cl_{\overline{0}}(V)
and, in the appropriate signatures,
\mathsf{S}_{1}
or
\mathsf{S}_{\pm}
are irreducible under the action of these groups. The induced action of
\mathfrak{so}(V)
on the (s)pinor modules is given by

A\cdot s=\pm\frac{1}{2}\omega_{A}\cdot s

(A.2)

for
A\in\mathfrak{so}(V)
and
s\in\mathsf{S}
, where the sign is the same as that in the Clifford relation (
A.1
) and the dot on the left-hand side is the Clifford action.

A.2
Lie derivatives

A standard concept in differential geometry, Lie derivatives play a central role in the definition of the Killing (super)algebra. In this appendix, we will first introduce some useful formulae for Lie derivatives of tensor fields and then show how these generalise to allow us to take Lie derivatives of spinor fields along Killing vectors.

A.2.1
Lie derivative of vectors and tensors

Let
(M,g)
be a connected pseudo-Riemannian manifold. We denote its Lie algebra of Killing vector fields
21
21

21

The notation should not be taken to imply that this is the Lie algebra of the isometry group of
(M,g)
which is in general only a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
; Killing vector fields may not integrate to a global action by a one-parameter group of isometries. We will not need to discuss the isometry group itself in this appendix or in the main text so this should not cause confusion.

by
\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
. The Levi-Civita connection will be denoted by
\nabla
. The bundle of skew-symmetric endomorphisms (with respect to
g
) of
TM
will appear when we discuss Killing vectors. We denote the space of sections of this bundle by
\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
and note that it is an (infinite-dimensional) Lie algebra under the commutator Lie bracket. The perspective presented here is due to Kostant
[
46
]
.

Given a vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, we define an endomorphism of
TM
by

A_{X}(Y):=-\nabla_{Y}X

for all
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. This gives us a useful formula for the Lie derivative along
X
: for
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
,

\mathscr{L}_{X}Y=\commutator{X}{Y}=\nabla_{X}Y-\nabla_{Y}X=\nabla_{X}Y+A_{X}Y,

(A.3)

where we have used the torsion-freeness of
\nabla
. We will also use

\commutator{X}{Y}=A_{X}Y-A_{Y}X.

(A.4)

A similar formula holds a tensor field
T
of any type

\mathscr{L}_{X}T=\nabla_{X}T+A_{X}\cdot T,

(A.5)

where the action of
A_{X}
on
T
is defined via a Leibniz formula. Note that we have a different Leibniz rule of the form

\nabla_{X}(A_{Y}\cdot T)=\nabla_{X}(A_{Y})\cdot T+A_{Y}\cdot\nabla_{X}T

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, or, abstracting
T
,
\commutator{\nabla_X}{A_Y}=\nabla_{X}A_{Y}
as endomorphisms of the space of tensor fields.

We can use the formula (
A.5
) to show that
X
is a Killing vector if and only if
A_{X}\in\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
: using the metric-compatibility of
\nabla
, we have
\mathscr{L}_{X}g=A_{X}\cdot g
, so

\mathscr{L}_{X}g=0\iff g(A_{X}Y,Z)+g(Y,A_{X}Z)=0,\qquad\forall Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M).

(A.6)

One can also show
[
46
, Lemma 2.2]
that, for
X\in\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
and
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
,

\nabla_{Y}A_{X}=-R(Y,X),

(A.7)

where
R
is the Riemann curvature, so in particular
R(Y,X)\in\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
. Then for two Killing vectors
X,Y\in\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
and a vector field
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, using equations (
A.4
) and (
A.7
) along with the algebraic Bianchi identity,

\displaystyle A_{\commutator{X}{Y}}Z

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{Z}\quantity(A_X Y - A_Y X)=-(\nabla_{Z}A_{X})Y-A_{X}\nabla_{Z}Y+(\nabla_{Z}A_{Y})X+A_{Y}\nabla_{Z}Y

\displaystyle=R(Z,X)Y+A_{X}A_{Y}Z-R(Z,Y)X-A_{Y}A_{X}Z=\commutator{A_X}{A_Y}Z-R(X,Y)Z.

We thus have

A_{\commutator{X}{Y}}=\commutator{A_X}{A_Y}-R(X,Y)

(A.8)

for all Killing vectors
X
and
Y
.

A.2.2
Spin structures and spinor bundles in arbitrary signature

Now let
(M,g)
be a pseudo-Riemannian spin manifold of signature
(p,q)
. We recall that this means that
M
is oriented and that there exists a
\Spin(p,q)
-principal bundle
P\to M
over
M
and a
\Spin(p,q)
-equivariant bundle map
\varpi\colon P\to F_{\rm SO}
, where
F_{\rm SO}\to M
is the special orthonormal frame bundle of
(M,g)
on which the spin group acts via the natural map
\Spin(p,q)\to\SO(p,q)
.

In indefinite signature, the structure group of the bundle
P\to M
can be reduced to
\Spin_{0}(p,q)
if and only if
(M,g)
is time-orientable, and if this is the case then
\varpi
restricts to a
\Spin_{0}(p,q)
-equivariant mapping
\varpi_{0}\colon P_{0}\to F_{SO_{0}}
of the reduced bundles. Note that many authors assume time-orientability and refer to this reduced structure as the spin structure.
22
22

22

The reduced structure has been called a
strong spin structure
, and manifolds admitting them
strongly spin

[
18
,
38
,
59
]
.

Let
S
be a (possibly
N
-extended) spinor module of
\Spin(p,q)
in the sense discussed in Section
2.1.2
and let
\underline{S}:=P\times_{\Spin(p,q)}S\to M
be the associated vector bundle over
M
, which we will call the
spinor bundle
. Sections of
\underline{S}
will be called
spinor fields
, and we denote the space of such fields by
\mathfrak{S}=\Gamma(\underline{S})
. We also let
\Cl(M,g)=P\times_{\Spin(p,q)}\Cl(p,q)\cong F_{\rm SO}\times_{\SO(p,q)}\Cl(p,q)
be the associated Clifford bundle (where the action is via the twisted adjoint, or the algebra automorphism induced by the natural action on
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
) and recall that we can identify it with the exterior bundle
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}T^{*}M
. Sections of this bundle can be Clifford-multiplied and Clifford-act on spinor fields via pointwise Clifford multiplication and action. The (spin-lift of) the Levi-Civita connection extends to all of these bundles, and there is a Leibniz rule for
\nabla
with respect to Clifford multiplication and action.

The adjoint bundle
\ad F_{\rm SO}\cong\ad P
can be identified with the space of
g
-skew-symmetric endomorphisms of
TM
, and it embeds in
\Cl(M,g)
as
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{2}TM
. As a Lie algebra, the space of sections
\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
acts on spinor fields via the Clifford action, i.e., we apply equation (
A.2
) pointwise.

A.2.3
Lie derivative of spinors

The formulae (
A.3
) and (
A.5
) suggest the following definition of the Lie derivative to spinor fields.

Definition A.1

(spinorial Lie derivative
[
45
]
)
.

The Lie derivative of a spinor field
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{S}=\Gamma(\underline{S})
along a Killing vector
X\in\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
is given by

\mathscr{L}_{X}\epsilon:=\nabla_{X}\epsilon+A_{X}\cdot\epsilon
.

Note, however, that while the formulae for the Lie derivatives of vector and tensor fields hold for the Lie derivative along any vector field
X
, the right-hand side of the spinor formula is only defined for Killing vectors
23
23

23

The spinorial Lie derivative is actually defined for
conformal Killing
vector fields in
[
45
]
, but we will only use it for Killing vectors here.

X
, since sections of
\End(TM)
do not act on
\mathfrak{S}
, while
\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
does. Note that
\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
is an (infinite-dimensional) Lie algebra and the action on
\mathfrak{S}
defines a representation. This action obeys a Leibniz rule of the form
\commutator{\nabla_X}{A}=\nabla_{X}A
as endomorphisms of
\mathfrak{S}
for all
A\in\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
; more explicitly,

\nabla_{X}(A\cdot\epsilon)=(\nabla_{X}A)\cdot\epsilon+A\cdot(\nabla_{X}\epsilon)

for all
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{S}
. It follows immediately from the above that we have yet another Leibniz rule
{\mathscr{L}_{X}(A\cdot\epsilon)=(\mathscr{L}_{X}A)\cdot\epsilon+A\cdot(\mathscr{L}_{X}\epsilon)}

for all
X\in\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
,
A\in\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
, or equivalently
{\commutator{\eL_X}{A}=\mathscr{L}_{X}A}
as endomorphisms of
\mathfrak{S}
. One can also show that where
X
is a Killing vector field and
Y
any vector field, the following compatibility condition between the spinorial Lie derivative and the Levi-Civita connection on spinors holds

\commutator{\eL_X}{\nabla_Y}=\nabla_{\commutator{X}{Y}},

(A.9)

while if both
X
and
Y
are Killing vector fields,

\mathscr{L}_{\commutator{X}{Y}}=\mathscr{L}_{X}\mathscr{L}_{Y}-\mathscr{L}_{Y}\mathscr{L}_{X}
.
In particular, the spinorial Lie derivative gives
\mathfrak{S}
the structure of an
\mathfrak{iso}(M,g)
module.

We can also use the Leibniz rule to define an action of
\mathfrak{so}(M,g)
, and thus a Lie derivative along Killing vectors, on sections of
\End\underline{S}
and on spaces of forms with values in this bundle
\Omega^{p}(M;\End\underline{S})
. More generally, the space of sections of any bundle constructed by taking duals and tensor products of
TM
and
\underline{S}
is equipped with such a structure, and all the Leibniz rules and compatibility conditions discussed above also hold when acting on these spaces.

A.3
Grading and filtration of superspaces and superalgebras

We assume that the reader is familiar with ordinary
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector spaces and with vector superspaces but recall and set some terminology for
\mathbb{Z}
-graded superspaces and algebras, and we also introduce filtrations.

A.3.1
Graded vector superspaces and superalgebras

A vector superspace
V
is said to be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
with grading
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{k}
if the latter is a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of
V
as a vector space and the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading is compatible with the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading on
V
in the sense that an element of degree
d
in the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading has degree (parity)
\overline{d}=d\mod 2
in the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading. Note that we can always make a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
vector space
into a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector
superspace
by taking the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading modulo
2
. We say that a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector (super)space
{V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{k}}
has
finite depth

h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
if
V_{-h}\neq 0
but
V_{k}=0
for all
k<-h
.

A Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}
is said to be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
with grading
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
if the latter is a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of
\mathfrak{g}
as a vector superspace and the superbracket respects this grading in the sense that
{\commutator{\fg_k}{\fg_l}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{k+l}}
.

If
V
is a vector superspace, we define the exterior algebra
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}V:=\mathcal{T}(V)/I
where
\mathcal{T}(V)
is the tensor algebra and I is the ideal generated by elements of the form
v\otimes w+(-1)^{\absolutevalue{v}\absolutevalue{w}}w\otimes v
, where
v
,
w
are homogeneous elements with parities
\absolutevalue{v}
,
\absolutevalue{w}
, respectively. This is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded associative algebra of depth 0: the grading is
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{\bullet}V=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{k}V
where
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{k}V
is the image of
{\mathcal{T}^{k}(V)=V^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigotimes}}{\bigotimes}{\bigotimes}{\bigotimes}k}}
under the quotient map. One can show that, for
V=V_{\overline{0}}\oplus V_{\overline{1}}
,

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{k}V=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{i}V_{\overline{0}}\otimes\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{j}V_{\overline{1}}.

(A.10)

It will occasionally be useful to remind the reader of this definition of
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigwedge}}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}^{k}
of a vector superspace; when we do so, we will say that we mean it in the “super-sense”. There is of course an analogous “super” version of the symmetric algebra
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\bigodot}}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}{\bigodot}^{\bullet}V
but we will not require it in this work.

A.3.2
Filtered vector spaces and algebras

We say that a vector space
V
is
\mathbb{Z}
-filtered
if there exists a decreasing sequence of vector subspaces
{V\supseteq\dots\supseteq V^{k-1}\supseteq V^{k}\supseteq V^{k+1}\supseteq\dots\supseteq 0}
.
To emphasise that we are taking
V
with its filtration, we will denote it by
V^{\bullet}
. We say that
V^{\bullet}
is
finite
if there exist some
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
such that
{V^{k_{1}}=V}
and
V^{k_{2}}=0
. Of course, if
V
is finite-dimensional then
V^{\bullet}
must be finite. We say that
V^{\bullet}
(not necessarily finite) has
finite depth

{h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
if
V_{-h}=V
but
V_{k}\neq V
for all
k>-h
.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}
-filtered vector space, there is an
associated graded vector space

\Gr V^{\bullet}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\Gr_{k}V,\qquad\text{where}\quad\Gr_{k}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.49501pt}{$\displaystyle{V^{k}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.49501pt}{$\displaystyle{V^{k+1}}$}}{\raisebox{4.24501pt}{$\textstyle{V^{k}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.24501pt}{$\textstyle{V^{k+1}}$}}{\raisebox{3.24501pt}{$\scriptstyle{V^{k}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.24501pt}{$\scriptstyle{V^{k+1}}$}}{\raisebox{2.45833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{V^{k}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.45833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{V^{k+1}}$}}}.

If the filtration
V^{\bullet}
is finite then so is the grading on
\Gr V^{\bullet}
, and if
V^{\bullet}
has finite depth
h
, so does
\Gr V^{\bullet}
. The associated graded vector space
\Gr V^{\bullet}
is (non-canonically) isomorphic to
V^{\bullet}
, which can be seen as follows. For each
V^{k}
, we choose a complement
W_{k}
to
V^{k+1}
so that
{V^{k}=W_{k}\oplus V^{k+1}}
. Then we have a grading
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}W_{k}
, and the quotient map
V^{k}\to\Gr_{k}V^{\bullet}
restricts to a linear isomorphism
W_{k}\to\Gr_{k}V^{\bullet}
. Taking the sum of these maps gives us a graded vector space isomorphism
V\to\Gr V^{\bullet}
.

If
V^{\bullet}
is equipped with a Lie algebra structure, then it is
\mathbb{Z}
-filtered as an algebra
if
